The Evolution of the National Commission for Backward Classes and the Architecture of OBC Reservation

The first National Commission for Backward Classes (NCBC) was constituted in 1993 under the National Commission for Backward Classes Act, 1993, pursuant to the Supreme Court’s direction in Indra Sawhney v. Union of India (1992). The Court had mandated a permanent body to examine requests for inclusion or exclusion of castes in the Central List of Other Backward Classes (OBCs) and to advise the Government on the “creamy layer” threshold. The 1993 Act provided for a Chairperson, a Vice‑Chairperson, and three Members, all appointed by the President.

A constitutional upgrade arrived with the 102nd Constitutional Amendment Act, 2018, which inserted Article 338B, granting the NCBC constitutional status akin to the National Commission for Scheduled Castes and Scheduled Tribes. Article 338B empowers the Commission to investigate and monitor all matters relating to the safeguards provided for socially and educationally backward classes, to inquire into specific complaints, and to present annual reports to the President. The Amendment also introduced Article 342A, requiring Presidential notification for the Central List of OBCs and mandating parliamentary approval for any subsequent modification.

The creamy layer criterion, initially set at an annual parental income of ₹1 lakh (1993), has been revised periodically — ₹2.5 lakh (2004), ₹4.5 lakh (2008), ₹6 lakh (2013), and ₹8 lakh (2017). The NCBC recommends revisions based on consumer price index movements and socio‑economic surveys. Its advice is binding on the Central Government for the Central List; states maintain their own State Backward Classes Commissions and lists, leading to a dual‑layer reservation architecture.

Judicial interpretation has refined the Commission’s powers. In National Commission for Backward Classes v. Union of India (2020), the Supreme Court held that the NCBC’s recommendation for inclusion of a caste in the Central List is not merely advisory; the Government must act on it within a reasonable time, failing which the Court may issue a mandamus. The Court also clarified that the “creamy layer” test applies only to OBCs, not to Scheduled Castes or Scheduled Tribes.

Federal dynamics are evident in the 2021 amendment to the NCBC Act, which mandated the creation of a National Database of OBCs, integrating data from state commissions, the Socio‑Economic and Caste Census (SECC), and the National Sample Survey. The database aims to rationalise the Central List, prevent duplication, and enable evidence‑based policy for sub‑categorisation — a proposal under active consideration by a Cabinet Committee. The interplay between the constitutional NCBC, state commissions, and the Ministry of Social Justice and Empowerment illustrates the layered governance of affirmative action in India.

The Development of the National Urban Renewal Mission (JNNURM) and the Smart Cities Mission as Instruments of Urban Integration

The Jawaharlal Nehru National Urban Renewal Mission (JNNURM) was launched in December 2005 as a Centrally Sponsored Scheme (CSS) with a seven‑year horizon (2005‑2012), later extended to 2014. It comprised two sub‑missions: Urban Infrastructure and Governance (UIG) for 65 mission cities, and Basic Services to the Urban Poor (BSUP) for all cities and towns. The Centre provided 50‑80 % of project costs as grants, contingent on states implementing mandatory reforms — enactment of community participation laws, public disclosure laws, e‑governance, and property tax reforms. JNNURM sanctioned 1,576 projects worth ₹1.02 lakh crore, of which 1,200 were completed, covering water supply, sewerage, solid waste management, urban transport, and housing for the urban poor.

The Smart Cities Mission (SCM), launched in June 2015, marked a shift from project‑based funding to a competitive, area‑based development model. One hundred cities were selected through a multi‑stage challenge; each city formed a Special Purpose Vehicle (SPV) — a corporate entity under the Companies Act, 2013 — with 50:50 equity between the State/ULB and the Centre. The Centre committed ₹48,000 crore over five years (₹500 crore per city), matched by states/ULBs. SCM focuses on “smart solutions” — integrated command and control centres, intelligent traffic management, e‑governance platforms, and renewable energy integration — within a defined “smart area” (retrofitting, redevelopment, or greenfield).

Both missions illustrate federal bargaining: JNNURM’s reform‑linked grants required state legislative action, while SCM’s SPV structure introduced a new governance layer that reports to a State‑level High Powered Steering Committee chaired by the Chief Minister. The Centre’s role is limited to fund release, capacity building through the National Institute of Urban Affairs (NIUA), and monitoring via the Smart Cities Dashboard. As of March 2024, 7,800 projects worth ₹1.9 lakh crore have been grounded under SCM, with 5,200 completed.

Critiques highlight implementation gaps: JNNURM’s reform conditionality was unevenly enforced; many states delayed enacting community participation laws. SCM’s area‑based approach has been accused of creating “islands of excellence” while neglecting city‑wide infrastructure. The 2021 Urban Transformation Agenda (UTA) seeks to merge lessons from both missions, proposing a unified “Urban Missions” framework with outcome‑based funding, a common data platform, and mandatory integration with the Atal Mission for Rejuvenation and Urban Transformation (AMRUT) for water and sewerage.

Federal integration is further deepened by the 15th Finance Commission’s allocation of ₹1.2 lakh crore as urban local body grants (2021‑2026), tied to performance benchmarks on property tax collection, audited accounts, and publication of service‑level benchmarks. This fiscal lever aligns central transfers with the reform trajectory initiated by JNNURM and SCM, reinforcing cooperative federalism in urban governance.

  • Confusing the constitutional status of NCM, NCW, and NCBC — only NCBC (since 2018) and NCSC/ST enjoy constitutional backing under Articles 338B, 338, 338A.
  • Assuming the Defence Acquisition Council can directly procure equipment; it only accords Acceptance of Necessity, while actual procurement follows the Defence Procurement Procedure executed by the Services and DDP.
  • Believing the Ordnance Factory Board still exists as a departmental entity; it was corporatised into seven DPSUs in October 2021.
  • Thinking JNNURM and Smart Cities Mission are simultaneous programmes; JNNURM ended in 2014, SCM began in 2015.
  • Assuming the creamy layer ceiling applies to SC/ST reservations; it applies exclusively to OBCs.
  • Stating that the National Commission for Minorities can enforce its recommendations on states; its recommendations are advisory, not binding.
  • Overlooking that the Smart Cities Mission’s SPV is a corporate body under the Companies Act, not a statutory authority, and its governance involves a 50:50 equity split between Centre and State/ULB.

The Evolution of the National Commission for Minorities and the National Commission for Women: Institutional Design, Mandate, and Federal Dynamics

The National Commission for Minorities (NCM) was constituted in 1993 under the National Commission for Minorities Act, 1992. It comprises a Chairperson, a Vice‑Chairperson and five other members, all appointed by the Central Government. The Act defines “minority” as communities notified under Section 2(c) — Muslims, Christians, Sikhs, Buddhists, Parsis and Jains. The NCM’s functions are advisory: it monitors the working of safeguards for minorities, makes recommendations for effective implementation, and submits annual reports to Parliament. Its recommendations are not binding on any government, a point repeatedly emphasised by the Supreme Court in State of Karnataka v. Union of India (2004).

The National Commission for Women (NCW) was set up in January 1992 under the National Commission for Women Act, 1990. It consists of a Chairperson, five members and a Member‑Secretary, all appointed by the Central Government. The NCW’s mandate covers review of constitutional and legal safeguards for women, recommendation of remedial legislative measures, facilitation of redressal of grievances, and promotion of research on women’s issues. Like the NCM, the NCW’s recommendations are advisory; however, the Commission has acquired de facto influence through its power to summon witnesses, receive evidence on affidavits and intervene in court proceedings as amicus curiae.

Both Commissions operate within a federal framework where states have constituted their own minority and women commissions. The Central Commissions coordinate with state bodies through periodic meetings and joint inquiries, but there is no constitutional hierarchy. The Ministry of Minority Affairs and the Ministry of Women and Child Development serve as nodal ministries, providing administrative support and forwarding reports to Parliament. Over the years, the NCM has been criticised for delayed appointments and limited enforcement teeth, while the NCW has been credited with catalysing the Sexual Harassment of Women at Workplace (Prevention, Prohibition and Redressal) Act, 2013, after its 1997 Vishaka guidelines intervention.

Comparative Snapshot of NCM and NCW
FeatureNational Commission for MinoritiesNational Commission for Women
Enabling ActNational Commission for Minorities Act, 1992National Commission for Women Act, 1990
CompositionChairperson, Vice‑Chairperson, 5 membersChairperson, 5 members, Member‑Secretary
Constitutional StatusStatutory (not constitutional)Statutory (not constitutional)
Binding Nature of RecommendationsAdvisory onlyAdvisory only
Key Landmark InterventionMonitoring post‑2002 Gujarat communal violenceVishaka guidelines (1997) → 2013 Act

The federal dynamics are further complicated by the fact that the President appoints the Chairpersons on the advice of the Council of Ministers, while state commissions are appointed by respective Governors. This dual appointment structure sometimes leads to political alignment concerns, especially when the Centre and a state are ruled by different parties. The 2022 amendment to the NCM Act introduced a provision for a fixed three‑year term for members, aiming to insulate the body from arbitrary removal, but the NCW Act has not been similarly amended. Both Commissions submit annual reports to Parliament, which are then laid before each House — a procedural safeguard that ensures legislative oversight but does not confer enforcement power.

The National Education Policy 2020 and the Restructuring of Higher Education Governance: Autonomy, Accreditation, and Federal Coordination

The National Education Policy (NEP) 2020, approved by the Union Cabinet on 29 July 2020, is the third major policy framework after 1968 and 1986. It envisions a transformative overhaul of the education sector from early childhood to higher education, with a target of achieving a 50 % Gross Enrolment Ratio (GER) in higher education by 2035. The policy proposes a single overarching regulator — the Higher Education Commission of India (HECI) — with four independent verticals: the National Higher Education Regulatory Council (NHERC) for regulation, the General Education Council (GEC) for standard‑setting, the Higher Education Grants Council (HEGC) for funding, and the National Accreditation Council (NAC) for accreditation. As of 2024, the HECI Bill has not been enacted; the University Grants Commission (UGC) and the All India Council for Technical Education (AICTE) continue to function under their existing statutes.

NEP 2020 introduces graded autonomy for institutions based on accreditation scores, a credit‑based framework (Academic Bank of Credits) enabling multiple entry and exit, and a mandate for multidisciplinary education by 2030. It also establishes the National Research Foundation (NRF) to fund competitive, peer‑reviewed research across disciplines and states, aiming to reduce the current concentration of research funding in a few central institutions. The policy emphasises federal coordination by proposing State Higher Education Councils (SHECs) in each state, which would align state university acts with the national framework while preserving the Concurrent List character of education.

Key implementation milestones include the launch of the National Education Technology Forum (NETF) in 2021 to facilitate digital infrastructure, the rollout of the National Assessment Centre (PARAKH) for standardised learning outcomes, and the introduction of a four‑year undergraduate programme with multiple exit options in select central universities from the 2023‑24 academic session. States such as Karnataka, Maharashtra and Tamil Nadu have enacted legislation to constitute SHECs, while others are in the consultation stage. The policy’s success hinges on the timely passage of the HECI Bill, adequate fiscal devolution to the HEGC, and the capacity of state governments to restructure affiliating university systems that currently govern over 90 % of colleges.

Examiner's Twist
A question asks: “Which of the following statements about the National Education Policy 2020 is correct? (a) It establishes a constitutional body for higher education regulation. (b) It replaces the UGC and AICTE with a single statutory regulator called HECI. (c) It proposes a National Research Foundation to fund research across states. (d) It makes education a Union List subject.”
Correct answer: (c). The NEP 2020 proposes the National Research Foundation (NRF) as a funding agency for research across states. It does not create a constitutional body (a), the HECI is only a proposed statutory regulator yet to be legislated (b), and education remains in the Concurrent List (d).
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question cites NEP 2020, first identify whether it refers to a proposal (yet to be legislated) or an implemented reform. Proposals like HECI, NRF, PARAKH are frequently tested as “envisaged” or “proposed”. Implemented steps such as the Academic Bank of Credits pilot, NETF launch, and four‑year UG programmes in central universities are factual. Distinguish between “statutory” and “constitutional” — NEP does not amend the Constitution.

The Indian Space Programme’s Second Phase: IN‑SPACe, the New Space Policy 2023, and Private Sector Integration

The Indian space programme, anchored by the Indian Space Research Organisation (ISRO) since 1969, entered a new phase in June 2020 when the Government announced the creation of the Indian National Space Promotion and Authorization Centre (IN‑SPACe) under the Department of Space. IN‑SPACe functions as a single‑window, independent nodal agency to authorise, promote and hand‑hold non‑governmental entities (NGEs) — private companies, start‑ups, academia — for end‑to‑end space activities including launch services, satellite manufacturing, ground stations and application services. The Centre is headed by a Chairman of the rank of Secretary to the Government of India and operates with a board comprising representatives from ISRO, the Department of Space, and independent experts.

The New Space Policy 2023, approved by the Cabinet on 6 April 2023, provides the overarching regulatory framework. Its four pillars are: (1) promotion of private sector participation in all segments of space activity; (2) creation of an enabling ecosystem through simplified licensing, access to ISRO facilities, and technology transfer; (3) capacity building via academia‑industry partnerships and skill development programmes; and (4) international cooperation aligned with the Artemis Accords and other multilateral frameworks. The policy designates NewSpace India Limited (NSIL), a Central Public Sector Enterprise under the Department of Space, as the commercial arm responsible for marketing launch services, satellite transponders and spin‑off technologies.

Since the policy’s notification, over 150 space start‑ups have registered with IN‑SPACe, and several have conducted sub‑orbital and orbital launches. Notable examples include Skyroot Aerospace’s Vikram‑S sub‑orbital launch (November 2022), Agnikul Cosmos’s Agnibaan‑SOrTeD test (March 2024), and Pixxel’s hyperspectral imaging satellites launched on ISRO’s PSLV. The policy also permits foreign direct investment (FDI) up to 100 % in satellite manufacturing and launch services under the automatic route, subject to security clearance. IN‑SPACe’s authorisation process follows a defined timeline — 30 days for preliminary review and 90 days for final decision — ensuring predictability for investors.

Federal dimensions arise because space is a Union subject (Entry 13 of the Union List), yet state governments host critical infrastructure: the Satish Dhawan Space Centre in Andhra Pradesh, the Liquid Propulsion Systems Centre in Tamil Nadu and Kerala, and the upcoming launch pad at Kulasekarapattinam in Tamil Nadu. State‑level single‑window clearances for land, water and power are coordinated through the respective State Space Cells, which liaise with IN‑SPACe. The policy explicitly encourages states to develop space industrial parks and incubators, with central financial assistance routed through the Department of Space.

The Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code 2016: A Unified Resolution Framework for Financial Integration across States

The Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code (IBC), 2016, enacted on 28 May 2016 and operational from 1 December 2016, replaced a fragmented regime comprising the Sick Industrial Companies (Special Provisions) Act, 1985, the Recovery of Debts Due to Banks and Financial Institutions Act, 1993, and the Securitisation and Reconstruction of Financial Assets and Enforcement of Security Interest Act, 2002. The Code provides a time‑bound, creditor‑driven resolution process: a corporate insolvency resolution process (CIRP) must be completed within 180 days, extendable by 90 days, failing which liquidation is ordered. The Insolvency and Bankruptcy Board of India (IBBI), established under Section 188, acts as the regulator for insolvency professionals, insolvency professional agencies and information utilities.

Adjudicatory authority rests with the National Company Law Tribunal (NCLT) and its appellate counterpart, the National Company Law Appellate Tribunal (NCLAT). NCLT benches are located in major cities across states — Delhi, Mumbai, Chennai, Kolkata, Ahmedabad, Bengaluru, Hyderabad, and others — ensuring geographic access. The Code applies to companies, limited liability partnerships, partnership firms and individuals (personal insolvency). Significant amendments have refined the framework: the 2018 amendment recognised homebuyers as financial creditors; the 2019 amendment raised the default threshold from ₹1 lakh to ₹1 crore; the 2020 ordinance suspended fresh insolvency filings for six months during the COVID‑19 pandemic; the 2021 amendment introduced a pre‑packaged insolvency resolution process for micro, small and medium enterprises (MSMEs); and the 2022 amendment incorporated a cross‑border insolvency framework aligned with the UNCITRAL Model Law.

As of March 2024, over 5,500 corporate insolvency cases have been admitted, with a resolution yield of approximately 32 % of admitted claims. The average resolution timeline has improved from 340 days in 2017‑18 to 210 days in 2022‑23. The Code’s federal character is evident in the uniform law across states, while the NCLT bench structure respects state‑level judicial infrastructure. The IBBI’s regulations on insolvency professional conduct, valuation standards and information utility operations are centrally framed but implemented through professionals registered across the country. The Code also interacts with state‑level revenue recovery laws, with the Supreme Court clarifying in Committee of Creditors of Essar Steel v. Satish Kumar Gupta (2019) that the IBC overrides inconsistent state enactments.

The Digital Personal Data Protection Act 2023: Federal Data Governance, Consent Architecture, and the Data Protection Board

The Digital Personal Data Protection Act (DPDP Act), 2023, received Presidential assent on 11 August 2023 after being passed by both Houses of Parliament. It governs the processing of digital personal data within India and applies extraterritorially to entities offering goods or services to data principals in India. The Act defines “personal data” as any data about an identifiable individual, and “processing” to include collection, storage, use, sharing and erasure. It establishes a consent‑centric framework: consent must be free, informed, specific, unconditional and withdrawable at any time. Data fiduciaries — entities determining the purpose and means of processing — are obligated to provide a clear notice, ensure data accuracy, implement security safeguards, and delete data upon purpose fulfilment or withdrawal of consent.

The Act creates the Data Protection Board of India (DPBI) as an adjudicatory body with powers to inquire into complaints, impose penalties up to ₹250 crore per instance, and direct remedial measures. The Board consists of a Chairperson and up to six members appointed by the Central Government on the recommendation of a selection committee comprising the Cabinet Secretary, the Secretary of the Ministry of Electronics and Information Technology, and an independent expert. The DPBI’s decisions are appealable to the Telecom Disputes Settlement and Appellate Tribunal (TDSAT) and thereafter to the Supreme Court.

Special provisions apply to “significant data fiduciaries” — entities processing large volumes of sensitive personal data or posing significant risk — who must appoint a Data Protection Officer, conduct Data Protection Impact Assessments, and undergo periodic audits. The Act permits cross‑border data transfers to countries notified by the Central Government as providing adequate protection, and empowers the Government to restrict transfers to jurisdictions deemed inadequate. Notably, the Act does not apply to non‑digital data, processing for personal or domestic purposes, or to certain state functions such as national security, public order, and law enforcement, which remain governed by separate statutes.

Federal dynamics are central: the DPDP Act is a Union law (Entry 94 of the Union List — “Central Bureau of Intelligence and Investigation” and related matters) and overrides any inconsistent state legislation on data protection. States do not have independent data protection authorities; however, state governments act as data fiduciaries for citizen databases (e.g., land records, health records) and must comply with the Act’s obligations. The Ministry of Electronics and Information Technology (MeitY) is the nodal ministry for rule‑making, and as of early 2025, the draft rules on consent managers, data breach notification timelines, and the functioning of the DPBI are under public consultation. The Act’s implementation will shape India’s digital economy, influencing sectors from fintech and e‑commerce to health‑tech and agritech, and will be a key reference point for future legislation on artificial intelligence governance.

  • Confusing the statutory nature of NCM and NCW with constitutional bodies — only the National Commission for Scheduled Castes, the National Commission for Scheduled Tribes, and the National Commission for Backward Classes (since 2018) enjoy constitutional status under Articles 338, 338A and 338B.
  • Assuming the HECI has already replaced UGC and AICTE — the Higher Education Commission of India remains a proposal; the UGC and AICTE continue to operate under their existing Acts.
  • Believing IN‑SPACe can directly launch satellites — IN‑SPACe authorises and facilitates private launches; actual launch services are provided by ISRO’s commercial arm NSIL or private launch operators.
  • Thinking the IBC applies only to companies — the Code covers LLPs, partnership firms and individuals (personal insolvency) in addition to companies.
  • Overlooking that the DPDP Act’s Data Protection Board is an adjudicatory body, not a regulatory authority — it does not frame regulations; rule‑making power rests with the Central Government through MeitY.
  • Stating that the New Space Policy 2023 permits 100 % FDI in all space activities — FDI up to 100 % is allowed in satellite manufacturing and launch services under the automatic route, but satellite broadcasting and ground‑segment services remain subject to sectoral caps.
  • Confusing the NEP 2020’s proposed National Research Foundation with the existing Science and Engineering Research Board — the NRF is a new entity envisaged to subsume and expand research funding across disciplines.

The Constitutional Evolution of the National Commission for Backward Classes: From Statutory Body to Constitutional Status (1993‑2018)

The National Commission for Backward Classes (NCBC) was first constituted under the National Commission for Backward Classes Act, 1993, as a statutory body tasked with examining requests for inclusion or exclusion of castes in the Central List of Other Backward Classes (OBCs) and advising the Union Government on the quantum of reservation. The Act gave the Commission the powers of a civil court while inquiring into complaints, but its recommendations were not binding. In 2000, the Supreme Court in Indra Sawhney v. Union of India (the Mandal judgment) directed the Government to constitute a permanent body to review the OBC lists periodically, reinforcing the need for an institutionalised mechanism.

The 102nd Constitutional Amendment Act, 2018, inserted Article 338B, granting the NCBC constitutional status. The Amendment also added Article 342A, empowering the President to notify the list of socially and educationally backward classes for each State and Union Territory, and requiring parliamentary approval for any subsequent modification. The Commission now consists of a Chairperson, a Vice‑Chairperson, and three other Members appointed by the President on the advice of the Council of Ministers. Its mandate includes investigating and monitoring all matters relating to the safeguards provided for backward classes under the Constitution, presenting annual reports to the President, and recommending measures for effective implementation of those safeguards.

The constitutional elevation altered the federal balance: the Centre now has a constitutionally mandated body to oversee OBC policy, while States retain the power to maintain their own State Lists of OBCs. However, the President’s notification under Article 342A creates a Central List that binds all States for purposes of central government jobs and admission to central educational institutions. The Commission’s advisory role on inclusion or exclusion of castes in the Central List has acquired greater weight, and its reports are laid before each House of Parliament, enhancing legislative scrutiny. The 2018 Amendment also introduced a new clause (338B(5)) requiring the Commission to consult the State Governments before tendering advice on matters affecting their lists, thereby institutionalising a consultative federal process.

Since 2018, the NCBC has examined several high‑profile demands, including the inclusion of the Maratha community in Maharashtra and the Jat community in several northern States. Its recommendations have been accepted in some cases and rejected in others, illustrating the political sensitivity of OBC classification. The Commission’s constitutional status also shields it from easy abolition or curtailment by ordinary legislation, a safeguard absent in the 1993 Act.

The Use and Misuse of Article 356: President's Rule, Judicial Review, and the S.R. Bommai Judgment (1994)

Article 356 empowers the President to assume the functions of a State Government on the report of the Governor or otherwise, if satisfied that a situation has arisen in which the Government of the State cannot be carried on in accordance with the provisions of the Constitution. Between 1950 and 1994, President’s Rule was imposed more than 100 times, often on political grounds such as loss of majority, coalition breakdowns, or to facilitate the installation of a preferred party. The most controversial instances include the dismissal of the Kerala Communist ministry in 1959, the imposition in Punjab in 1987, and the repeated use in Uttar Pradesh and Bihar during the 1990s.

The Supreme Court’s landmark judgment in S.R. Bommai v. Union of India (1994) laid down stringent judicial review standards. A nine‑judge Bench held that the proclamation under Article 356 is subject to judicial scrutiny on the grounds of mala fide, irrelevance, or non‑existence of material. The Court ruled that the President’s satisfaction must be based on objective material, not on the Governor’s subjective report alone. It also mandated that the State Legislative Assembly cannot be dissolved until Parliament approves the proclamation, and that the Centre must give the State Government an opportunity to prove its majority on the floor of the House before imposing President’s Rule.

Post‑Bommai, the frequency of Article 356 impositions declined sharply. The Court’s guidelines were codified in the 1999 Rameshwar Prasad v. Union of India decision, which reiterated that the Governor’s report must be based on “objective and verifiable” facts. The 2005 State of Rajasthan v. Union of India case further clarified that the President’s proclamation must be accompanied by a detailed statement of reasons. Despite these safeguards, political controversies persisted, notably the 2016 imposition in Uttarakhand and the 2018 imposition in Jammu & Kashmir (later reorganised into Union Territories). In each case, the Supreme Court intervened to restore elected governments or to demand floor tests, reinforcing the Bommai framework.

The judgment also influenced the Sarkaria Commission (1988) and the Punchhi Commission (2010) recommendations, both of which advocated for stricter procedural safeguards, including a mandatory floor test before dissolution and a time‑bound parliamentary approval. The 2018 amendment to the Government of India (Transaction of Business) Rules, which requires the Union Home Ministry to record detailed reasons before recommending President’s Rule, reflects the enduring impact of the Bommai doctrine on executive practice.

Examiner's trap
A question asks: “Under Article 356, the President can impose President’s Rule on the basis of the Governor’s report alone. True or False?”
False. The S.R. Bommai (1994) judgment held that the President’s satisfaction must be based on objective material and not merely on the Governor’s subjective report. The proclamation is subject to judicial review for mala fide, irrelevance, or absence of material. Moreover, the State Assembly cannot be dissolved before Parliament approves the proclamation, and a floor test must be offered to the incumbent government.
Exam‑day strategy
When a question on Article 356 appears, first recall the Bommai principles: (1) objective material, (2) judicial review, (3) no dissolution before parliamentary approval, (4) floor test. Use the mnemonic “O‑J‑N‑F” (Objective, Judicial, No dissolution, Floor test) to structure a concise answer. Cite the year 1994 and the nine‑judge Bench to score full marks.

The Transition from Planning Commission to NITI Aayog: Redefining Cooperative Federalism (2015‑Present)

The Planning Commission, established by a Cabinet resolution in 1950, functioned as the apex body for formulating Five‑Year Plans and allocating central assistance to States. Its composition — chaired by the Prime Minister with the Finance Minister and other Ministers as members — gave the Union Government decisive control over resource distribution, often criticised as a “centralised planning” model that marginalised State priorities. The Commission’s Deputy Chairman wielded significant influence over sectoral allocations, and the National Development Council (NDC) served only as a consultative forum.

In 2014, the newly elected Government announced the replacement of the Planning Commission with the National Institution for Transforming India (NITI) Aayog, formally constituted on 1 January 2015 through a Cabinet resolution. NITI Aayog’s Governing Council comprises the Prime Minister (Chairperson), all Chief Ministers, Lieutenant Governors of Union Territories, and select Union Ministers. This structure institutionalises a “Team India” approach, giving States a direct voice in policy formulation. The Vice‑Chairperson, appointed by the Prime Minister, acts as the functional head, while a Chief Executive Officer manages day‑to‑day operations.

Key functional shifts include: (1) replacement of Five‑Year Plans with a 15‑year Vision Document, a 7‑year Strategy Paper, and a 3‑year Action Agenda; (2) emphasis on “cooperative federalism” through the Governing Council’s regular meetings, where States present their development priorities; (3) creation of specialised verticals (e.g., Agriculture, Health, Education, Digital Economy) that work with State counterparts; (4) introduction of the “Aspirational Districts Programme” (2018) targeting 112 under‑developed districts through real‑time data dashboards monitored jointly by Centre and States; (5) establishment of the “NITI Aayog Innovation Index” and “State Health Index” to foster competitive federalism.

Critics argue that NITI Aayog lacks statutory backing and financial powers; it cannot allocate funds, which remain with the Finance Ministry and the Finance Commission. Its recommendations are advisory, and the absence of a constitutional mandate limits enforceability. Nevertheless, the platform has facilitated high‑level dialogue on contentious issues such as GST compensation, water sharing, and the implementation of centrally sponsored schemes. The 2022 Governing Council meeting, for instance, produced a consensus on the “National Education Policy 2020” implementation roadmap, illustrating the body’s potential to harmonise Centre‑State policy.

The Role of the Central Information Commission and the Right to Information Act in Strengthening Federal Transparency (2005‑Present)

The Right to Information Act, 2005 (RTI Act) came into force on 12 October 2005, establishing a statutory regime for citizens to access information held by public authorities. The Act created the Central Information Commission (CIC) at the Union level and mandated the establishment of State Information Commissions (SICs) in each State. The CIC consists of a Chief Information Commissioner and up to ten Information Commissioners, appointed by the President on the recommendation of a committee comprising the Prime Minister, the Leader of Opposition in the Lok Sabha, and a Union Cabinet Minister nominated by the Prime Minister.

The RTI Act applies to all “public authorities” defined broadly to include bodies established by the Constitution, by law made by Parliament or State Legislatures, and bodies substantially financed by the Government. This definition covers Union Ministries, State Departments, Panchayati Raj Institutions, Urban Local Bodies, and even certain NGOs receiving substantial government funding. The Act prescribes a 30‑day timeline for providing information (48 hours for life‑and‑liberty matters) and provides for penalties on Public Information Officers (PIOs) for unjustified delays or malafide denial.

Federal dynamics are embedded in the appellate structure: a first appeal lies with a senior officer within the same public authority; a second appeal lies with the CIC (for Union authorities) or the respective SIC (for State authorities). The CIC and SICs have the powers of a civil court to summon witnesses, require production of documents, and impose penalties up to ₹25,000 per day of delay, subject to a maximum of ₹25,000. Their decisions are binding, though a writ petition under Article 226/227 can be filed in the High Court.

Since 2005, the CIC has delivered landmark orders expanding the scope of “public authority” to include political parties (2013 order, later stayed by the Supreme Court), the Office of the Chief Justice of India (2019), and the Prime Minister’s Office (2020). The SICs have similarly compelled disclosure of land records, ration card data, and police complaint registers, empowering citizens to monitor local governance. However, the 2019 amendment to the RTI Act, which empowered the Central Government to unilaterally determine the tenure, salary, and conditions of service of Information Commissioners, raised concerns about the independence of the CIC and SICs. The Supreme Court in Anjali Bhardwaj v. Union of India (2021) upheld the amendment but emphasised that the selection process must remain transparent and merit‑based.

The RTI regime has also influenced inter‑governmental relations: States often cite the Act to demand information from Central agencies (e.g., CBI, NIA) for cooperative investigations, while the Centre uses RTI disclosures to monitor State implementation of centrally sponsored schemes. The annual reports of the CIC and SICs, laid before Parliament and State Legislatures respectively, serve as institutional checks on executive opacity across the federation.

The Evolution of the National Commission for Minorities and the National Commission for Women: Statutory Mandates, Powers, and Federal Implications

The National Commission for Minorities (NCM) was constituted under the National Commission for Minorities Act, 1992, following the recommendation of the 1989‑90 Minorities Commission. The Act defines “minority” as a community notified by the Central Government; initially five religious communities — Muslims, Christians, Sikhs, Buddhists, and Parsis — were notified in 1993, and Jains were added in 2014. The NCM comprises a Chairperson, a Vice‑Chairperson, and five Members, all appointed by the Central Government. Its functions include evaluating the progress of minority development, monitoring the working of safeguards in the Constitution and laws, and making recommendations for effective implementation.

The National Commission for Women (NCW) was established under the National Commission for Women Act, 1990, with a Chairperson, five Members, and a Member‑Secretary appointed by the Central Government. Its mandate covers reviewing constitutional and legal safeguards for women, recommending remedial legislative measures, facilitating redressal of grievances, and advising the Government on policy matters affecting women. Both Commissions are statutory bodies; they do not enjoy constitutional status under Articles 338, 338A, or 338B, a distinction frequently tested in examinations.

Federal implications arise because the NCM and NCW operate at the Union level while States have their own Minorities Commissions and Women Commissions (e.g., Maharashtra State Minorities Commission, Kerala State Women’s Commission). The Union Commissions can inquire into matters falling within the Union List or Concurrent List, but their jurisdiction over purely State subjects is limited. They often rely on State governments for data and cooperation, leading to occasional friction. For instance, the NCM’s 2018 report on the status of minorities in Assam highlighted discrepancies between Central and State data on the National Register of Citizens, prompting a joint review mechanism.

Both Commissions submit annual reports to the Central Government, which are laid before Parliament. Their recommendations have influenced legislation such as the Muslim Women (Protection of Rights on Marriage) Act, 2019 (triple talaq), and the Criminal Law (Amendment) Act, 2013 (post‑Nirbhaya reforms). However, the absence of constitutional protection means their composition, powers, and even existence can be altered by a simple majority in Parliament, a vulnerability highlighted during the 2020 proposal to merge the NCM with the National Commission for Backward Classes — a move that was later dropped after stakeholder opposition.

In practice, the NCM and NCW function as watchdogs and advisory bodies, leveraging their statutory powers to summon officials, conduct inquiries, and recommend policy changes. Their effectiveness depends on political will, resource allocation, and the willingness of State governments to implement recommendations. The federal architecture thus features a layered system: Union‑level statutory commissions, State‑level counterparts, and constitutional bodies for SCs, STs, and OBCs, each with distinct mandates and varying degrees of autonomy.

  • Confusing the statutory nature of NCM and NCW with constitutional bodies — only the National Commission for Scheduled Castes, the National Commission for Scheduled Tribes, and the National Commission for Backward Classes (since 2018) enjoy constitutional status under Articles 338, 338A and 338B.
  • Assuming Article 356 can be invoked solely on the Governor’s report — the S.R. Bommai (1994) judgment requires objective material and judicial review, and mandates a floor test before dissolution.
  • Believing NITI Aayog has financial allocation powers — it is an advisory think‑tank; fund allocation remains with the Finance Ministry and the Finance Commission.
  • Thinking the RTI Act’s 2019 amendment removed the independence of Information Commissioners — the amendment changed tenure and salary determination but the Supreme Court upheld the selection process’s transparency requirement.
  • Overlooking that the President’s notification under Article 342A creates a Central OBC List binding on all States for central jobs and admissions, while States maintain separate State Lists.
  • Stating that the Planning Commission was a constitutional body — it was created by a Cabinet resolution in 1950 and had no constitutional backing.
  • Confusing the Central Information Commission’s jurisdiction with that of State Information Commissions — the CIC hears appeals against Union authorities; SICs hear appeals against State authorities.

The 73rd and 74th Constitutional Amendments: Institutionalising Panchayati Raj and Urban Local Bodies as Pillars of Democratic Decentralisation

The Constitution (73rd Amendment) Act, 1992, passed in December 1992 and brought into force on 24 April 1993, inserted Part IX (Articles 243‑243O) and the Eleventh Schedule listing 29 subjects for devolution to panchayats. The Constitution (74th Amendment) Act, 1992, effective 1 June 1993, added Part IXA (Articles 243P‑243ZG) and the Twelfth Schedule with 18 municipal functions. Both amendments mandate a three‑tier panchayat structure — Gram Panchayat at village level, Panchayat Samiti at intermediate level, and Zila Parishad at district level — and a corresponding urban hierarchy of Nagar Panchayats, Municipal Councils and Municipal Corporations. Reservations are constitutionally guaranteed: seats for Scheduled Castes and Scheduled Tribes in proportion to their population (Article 243D/243T) and not less than one‑third of all seats for women, including the offices of chairpersons. State Election Commissions (Article 243K/243ZA) supervise elections, while State Finance Commissions (Article 243I/243Y) recommend fiscal transfers. The Provisions of the Panchayats (Extension to Scheduled Areas) Act, 1996 (PESA) extends Part IX to Fifth Schedule areas with modifications such as Gram Sabha ownership of minor forest produce. The Ministry of Panchayati Raj was created in 2004 to coordinate implementation. Landmark Supreme Court rulings include K. Krishnamurthy v. Union of India (1995) upholding reservation validity, State of Uttar Pradesh v. Rajesh Gautam (2005) affirming devolution of funds, and Union of India v. R. Gandhi (2010) protecting State Election Commission independence. As of 2023, India has 2,55,000 Gram Panchayats, 6,500 intermediate panchayats and 650 district panchayats with over 31 lakh elected representatives, 46 % women. The Fourteenth Finance Commission (2015‑20) recommended ₹2.87 lakh crore in grants to local bodies; the Fifteenth Finance Commission (2021‑26) raised this to ₹4.36 lakh crore. Mission Antyodaya (2017) and the mandatory Gram Panchayat Development Plan (GPDP) from 2018 aim at convergent planning. Persistent challenges include inadequate own‑source revenue, parallel state‑level bodies, bureaucratic resistance, and delayed elections in several states.

The Digital India Programme and BharatNet: Building a National Digital Infrastructure for Governance Integration

Launched on 1 July 2015, the Digital India programme rests on three pillars: digital infrastructure as a utility to every citizen, governance and services on demand, and digital empowerment of citizens. Its backbone, BharatNet (formerly the National Optical Fibre Network), was approved in October 2011. Phase I (2014‑2017) connected 1 lakh Gram Panchayats with optical fibre; Phase II (2017‑2023) targets the remaining 1.5 lakh using an optimal mix of fibre, radio and satellite. Implementation is by Bharat Broadband Network Limited (BBNL), a public sector undertaking under the Department of Telecommunications, funded through the Universal Service Obligation Fund (USOF). Common Service Centres (CSCs) serve as last‑mile access points; over 5 lakh CSCs were operational by 2023. The predecessor National e‑Governance Plan (NeGP, 2006) comprised 31 Mission Mode Projects, many of which were subsumed into Digital India. Key platforms include Aadhaar (UIDAI), DigiLocker (2015), e‑Sign (2015), UMANG (2017), the National Scholarship Portal, and e‑NAM (2016) for agricultural marketing. The National Knowledge Network (NKN) links 1,700+ research and education institutions. The Digital India Land Records Modernisation Programme (DILRMP, 2008) and PM‑WANI (2020) for public Wi‑Fi via Public Data Office Aggregators further extend reach. As of March 2023, 1.3 lakh Gram Panchayats have optical fibre connectivity against a 2.5 lakh target. Challenges persist in right‑of‑way clearances, state‑level coordination, financial sustainability of CSCs, and digital literacy gaps, especially in tribal and remote regions.

Examiner's Twist: Overlapping Mandates
A question asks: 'Which agency is the nodal body for cyber incident response in India, and how does its mandate differ from that of the National Critical Information Infrastructure Protection Centre?'
The Indian Computer Emergency Response Team (CERT‑In), established under Section 70B of the IT Act 2000, is the national nodal agency for cyber incident response, coordination, and awareness across all sectors. The National Critical Information Infrastructure Protection Centre (NCIIPC), created in 2014 under the National Technical Research Organisation, has a sector‑specific mandate to protect designated critical information infrastructure — power, transport, banking, telecom, defence, and space. CERT‑In’s role is cross‑sectoral incident handling and advisory; NCIIPC’s role is proactive protection, threat assessment, and resilience building for identified critical sectors. The distinction lies in statutory source (IT Act vs. NTRO charter), reporting line (Ministry of Electronics and Information Technology vs. National Security Adviser), and operational focus (reactive coordination vs. proactive infrastructure hardening).
Exam-Day Strategy
When comparing institutional mandates, tabulate the statutory source, reporting line, and sectoral focus; this prevents conflating CERT‑In’s all‑sector incident coordination with NCIIPC’s critical‑infrastructure protection.

The Pradhan Mantri Jan Dhan Yojana, Direct Benefit Transfer, and the National Payments Corporation of India: Financial Inclusion as Federal Integration

The Pradhan Mantri Jan Dhan Yojana (PMJDY) was launched on 28 August 2014 to provide universal banking access. It offers zero‑balance accounts, a RuPay debit card, accidental insurance cover of ₹1 lakh, life cover of ₹30,000, and an overdraft facility up to ₹10,000 after six months of satisfactory operation. By March 2024, 48.7 crore accounts had been opened, 55 % held by women and 67 % in rural areas. Direct Benefit Transfer (DBT) began on 1 January 2013 with the PAHAL scheme for LPG subsidy, using Aadhaar‑linked bank accounts; it now covers over 300 schemes including MGNREGA wages, scholarships, pensions, and PM‑KISAN. The Comptroller and Auditor General estimated DBT saved ₹2.2 lakh crore in leakages by 2022. The National Payments Corporation of India (NPCI), incorporated in December 2008 under the Payment and Settlement Systems Act 2007, operates RuPay (2012), Immediate Payment Service (IMPS, 2010), Unified Payments Interface (UPI, launched April 2016), Bharat Interface for Money (BHIM, 2016), and Aadhaar Enabled Payment System (AePS, 2016). UPI monthly volume crossed 10 billion transactions in August 2023, with a value of ₹15.5 lakh crore. The Reserve Bank of India regulates payment systems through its Board for Regulation and Supervision of Payment and Settlement Systems. The RBI’s Financial Inclusion Index rose from 43.4 (2017) to 60.1 (2023). Complementary schemes include the Pradhan Mantri Mudra Yojana (2015) for micro‑credit up to ₹10 lakh and Stand‑Up India (2016) for SC/ST and women entrepreneurs. The Jan Dhan Darshak GIS app maps banking touchpoints. Challenges remain: a significant share of dormant accounts, last‑mile connectivity in remote areas, fraud in AePS transactions, and the need for sustained financial literacy programmes.

The National Action Plan on Climate Change and State Action Plans: Cooperative Federalism in Environmental Governance

The National Action Plan on Climate Change (NAPCC) was released on 30 June 2008 by the Prime Minister’s Council on Climate Change. It comprises eight national missions: National Solar Mission (target 100 GW by 2022, revised to 280 GW by 2030), National Mission for Enhanced Energy Efficiency (Perform, Achieve and Trade scheme), National Mission on Sustainable Habitat, National Water Mission, National Mission for Sustaining the Himalayan Ecosystem, National Mission for a Green India (afforestation of 5 million hectares), National Mission for Sustainable Agriculture, and National Mission on Strategic Knowledge for Climate Change. Each mission has a nodal ministry. States were directed to prepare State Action Plans on Climate Change (SAPCC) aligned with NAPCC; by 2015, 33 states and union territories had submitted plans. The Ministry of Environment, Forest and Climate Change (MoEFCC) coordinates implementation through a National Steering Committee chaired by the Prime Minister. The National Green Tribunal (NGT), established under the NGT Act 2010, provides expeditious disposal of environmental cases. India’s Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC) under the Paris Agreement (2015) commits to reducing emissions intensity by 33‑35 % by 2030 from 2005 levels and achieving 40 % non‑fossil fuel power capacity. As of 2023, installed solar capacity stands at 70 GW and wind at 44 GW. The National Clean Air Programme (NCAP, 2019) targets a 20‑30 % reduction in PM2.5 in 132 cities by 2024. The National Adaptation Fund for Climate Change (NAFCC), operational since 2015, has an allocation of ₹350 crore. State Climate Change Cells have been set up in 28 states. The International Solar Alliance (ISA), launched in 2015 and headquartered in Gurugram, facilitates solar technology transfer. Key challenges include financing gaps, inter‑state water disputes affecting adaptation, limited capacity of municipal bodies, and data deficits for vulnerability assessment.

The Evolution of India’s Cyber Security Architecture: CERT‑In, NCIIPC, and the National Cyber Security Policy

The Indian Computer Emergency Response Team (CERT‑In) was established in January 2004 under Section 70B of the Information Technology Act 2000 as the national nodal agency for cyber incident response, coordination, and awareness. The National Critical Information Infrastructure Protection Centre (NCIIPC) was created on 16 January 2014 under the National Technical Research Organisation to protect designated critical sectors — power, transport, banking, telecom, defence, and space. The National Cyber Security Policy (NCSP) 2013 articulated 14 objectives, including a secure cyber ecosystem, workforce development, and public‑private partnership. The Information Technology (Amendment) Act 2008 introduced Sections 66F (cyber terrorism), 67C (preservation of information), and enhanced penalties. The Cyber Swachhta Kendra (Botnet Cleaning and Malware Analysis Centre) was launched in February 2017 by CERT‑In in collaboration with Internet Service Providers. The National Cyber Coordination Centre (NCCC), operationalised in 2017, performs metadata analysis and situational awareness. The Indian Cyber Crime Coordination Centre (I4C), under the Ministry of Home Affairs, was established in 2018 to handle cybercrime investigation, capacity building, and citizen reporting via the National Cyber Crime Reporting Portal. The Personal Data Protection Bill 2019 was withdrawn; the Digital Personal Data Protection Act 2023 was enacted in August 2023, establishing the Data Protection Board of India. The Supreme Court in Justice K.S. Puttaswamy (Retd.) v. Union of India (2017) declared privacy a fundamental right under Article 21, shaping data governance. National Crime Records Bureau data show cybercrime cases rose 63 % in 2022 (65,893 cases), with fraud, sexual exploitation, and ransomware as major categories. Notable incidents include the AIIMS ransomware attack (November 2022) and the Power Grid cyber intrusion (2021). The Cyber Surakshit Bharat initiative (2018) trains government officials. Persistent challenges include jurisdictional overlap among CERT‑In, NCIIPC, and I4C; shortage of skilled personnel; evolving state‑sponsored threat actors; and balancing surveillance imperatives with privacy rights.

  • Confusing the 73rd Amendment’s one‑third women’s reservation with the 74th Amendment’s identical provision but forgetting that the 74th also mandates reservation for SC/ST in municipalities, not for chairpersons.
  • Assuming BharatNet is fully completed; Phase II is ongoing and many Gram Panchayats still lack last‑mile connectivity.
  • Treating PMJDY accounts as automatically active for DBT; dormant accounts and incomplete Aadhaar seeding often break the transfer chain.
  • Equating the National Action Plan on Climate Change with a legally binding instrument; it is a policy framework, and State Action Plans are not uniformly enforceable.
  • Believing CERT‑In has enforcement powers over critical infrastructure; its role is advisory and coordination, while NCIIPC holds the protection mandate for designated critical sectors.
  • Overlooking that the Digital Personal Data Protection Act 2023 applies only to digital personal data, not offline data, and that the Data Protection Board is yet to be fully constituted.
  • Ignoring the distinction between the National Cyber Coordination Centre’s metadata analysis and the Indian Cyber Crime Coordination Centre’s investigation and citizen‑reporting functions.

The Use of Article 356 (President's Rule) and the S.R. Bommai Judgment: Federalism and State Integration (1950‑1994)

Constitutional Basis and Early Practice

Article 356 empowers the President to assume the functions of a state government when the constitutional machinery in that state fails. The provision was modelled on Section 93 of the Government of India Act 1935 and was intended as an emergency safeguard. In the first two decades after independence the Centre invoked it sparingly — Kerala (1959), Punjab (1966) and Uttar Pradesh (1968) being early instances — but the political turbulence of the 1970s and 1980s saw a sharp rise. By 1994 President's Rule had been imposed more than one hundred times, often on the advice of a Union Cabinet dominated by the ruling party at the Centre.

Frequency and Political Context

The 1977 Janata government used Article 356 to dismiss nine Congress‑ruled state governments in a single day, a move that triggered the first major judicial scrutiny. The 1980s witnessed repeated dismissals in Punjab, Assam, Jammu & Kashmir and the north‑eastern states, frequently justified on grounds of law‑and‑order breakdown or loss of majority. Critics argued that the provision became a tool for partisan centralisation, undermining the federal bargain envisaged in the Constitution.

The S.R. Bommai Judgment (1994)

In S.R. Bommai v. Union of India (1994) a nine‑judge bench of the Supreme Court laid down binding guidelines: (1) the proclamation must be based on objective material, not on the subjective satisfaction of the Governor; (2) the state legislative assembly cannot be dissolved before Parliament approves the proclamation; (3) the Centre must give the state government an opportunity to prove its majority on the floor of the House; (4) judicial review is available on the ground of mala fides or irrelevance. The judgment converted Article 356 from a virtually unchecked executive power into a justiciable constitutional provision.

Post‑Bommai Developments

After 1994 the frequency of President's Rule dropped dramatically. The few later instances — such as Uttar Pradesh (1995), Bihar (2005) and Arunachal Pradesh (2016) — were subjected to intense judicial scrutiny, and in several cases the Supreme Court restored the dismissed governments. The judgment also reinforced the principle that the Governor's report must be based on verifiable facts, not on political expediency.

Examiner’s Twist

Questions often ask whether the Bommai judgment "amended" Article 356. The correct answer is that it interpreted the article and imposed procedural safeguards; the text of Article 356 remains unchanged. Another trap is to confuse the Governor's discretionary power under Article 163 with the President's power under Article 356 — they operate at different levels and are subject to different judicial standards.

The Evolution of the Collegium System and Judicial Appointments: From the First Judges Case to the National Judicial Appointments Commission (1993‑2015)

First Judges Case (1981): Executive Primacy

In S.P. Gupta v. Union of India (1981) the Supreme Court held that the President (on the advice of the Council of Ministers) had primacy in the appointment of judges, and that the Chief Justice of India's opinion was merely consultative. This entrenched executive control over the higher judiciary for over a decade.

Second Judges Case (1993): Birth of the Collegium

The nine‑judge bench in Supreme Court Advocates‑on‑Record Association v. Union of India (1993) reversed the 1981 position. It declared that the Chief Justice of India must have primacy, and that appointments would be made by a collegium comprising the CJI and the two senior‑most judges of the Supreme Court. For High Courts, the collegium consisted of the Chief Justice of the High Court and its two senior‑most judges.

Third Judges Case (1998): Composition and Procedure

A presidential reference under Article 143 led to the Third Judges Case (1998), which expanded the Supreme Court collegium to the CJI and four senior‑most judges, and mandated written reasons for each recommendation. It also introduced the requirement of consultation with senior judges of the concerned High Court for High Court appointments.

The NJAC Act (2014) and its Striking Down (2015)

Parliament enacted the National Judicial Appointments Commission Act, 2014, and the 99th Constitutional Amendment, creating a six‑member body (CJI, two senior Supreme Court judges, the Law Minister, and two eminent persons). In Supreme Court Advocates‑on‑Record Association v. Union of India (2015) a five‑judge bench struck down both the Act and the Amendment, holding that they violated the basic structure by compromising judicial independence and the primacy of the judiciary in appointments.

Current Framework and Critiques

The collegium system continues, with the Memorandum of Procedure (MoP) governing its functioning. Critics point to opacity, lack of accountability, and delays; proponents argue it insulates the judiciary from executive pressure. The Supreme Court has periodically issued directions to improve transparency, such as publishing collegium resolutions on its website.

Examiner’s Twist

A common error is to state that the NJAC was struck down because it gave the executive a veto. The Court's ratio was that any composition that dilutes the primacy of the CJI and the judiciary's collective opinion violates the basic structure, irrespective of veto powers. Another trap is to conflate the collegium with the "judicial independence" doctrine — the collegium is a procedural mechanism, not a constitutional article.

Case‑law mapping
Explain the constitutional basis of the collegium system and why the National Judicial Appointments Commission (NJAC) was struck down by the Supreme Court in 2015.
The collegium system derives from the Supreme Court's interpretation of Articles 124(2) and 217(1) in the Second Judges Case (1993), which held that "consultation" with the Chief Justice of India means "concurrence", giving the judiciary primacy in appointments. The Third Judges Case (1998) refined the composition and procedure. The NJAC Act, 2014 and the 99th Amendment sought to replace the collegium with a mixed body including the Law Minister and two eminent persons. In the Fourth Judges Case (2015) the Court held that the NJAC violated the basic structure because it undermined the primacy of the judiciary and judicial independence, which are part of the Constitution's basic structure. The Court reiterated that the executive cannot have a decisive role in judicial appointments, and struck down both the Act and the Amendment.
Exam‑day strategy
Memorise the chronological chain: First Judges Case (1981) → Second Judges Case (1993) → Third Judges Case (1998) → NJAC Act (2014) → Fourth Judges Case (2015). Use the mnemonic "1981‑Executive, 1993‑Collegium, 1998‑Expansion, 2014‑NJAC, 2015‑StruckDown". In answer writing, always cite the case name and year; the examiner awards marks for precise case‑law references.

The Role of the Union Public Service Commission and State Public Service Commissions in Administrative Integration (1950‑Present)

Constitutional Provisions (Articles 315‑323)

Articles 315 to 323 of the Constitution provide for a Union Public Service Commission (UPSC) and a State Public Service Commission (SPSC) for each state. The UPSC is the central recruiting agency for All India Services (IAS, IPS, IFS) and Central Services (Group A and B). SPSCs perform the analogous function for state services. The President appoints the Chairman and members of the UPSC; the Governor does so for SPSCs. Their independence is secured by fixed tenure (six years or until age 65 for UPSC, 62 for SPSC) and removal only on grounds of misbehaviour after a Supreme Court inquiry.

Recruitment to All India Services and Central Services

The UPSC conducts the Civil Services Examination (CSE), the Indian Forest Service Examination, the Engineering Services Examination, and the Combined Defence Services Examination. Successful candidates are allocated to cadres jointly controlled by the Centre and the states, ensuring a unified administrative backbone. The UPSC also advises the President on disciplinary matters, promotions, and service rules for All India Services officers.

State PSCs and the Federal Balance

Each SPSC conducts examinations for state civil services, police services, and other state‑level posts. The Constitution permits the UPSC to assist a state PSC on request (Article 321), and two or more states may agree to have a Joint State PSC (Article 315). This flexibility supports administrative integration while respecting state autonomy.

Joint Recruitment and the Combined Graduate Level Examination

Since 1975 the Staff Selection Commission (SSC) — a body created by executive resolution — conducts the Combined Graduate Level (CGL) examination for subordinate posts in central ministries. Although not a constitutional body, the SSC operates under the overall supervision of the Department of Personnel and Training, complementing the UPSC's role in middle‑level recruitment.

Recent Reforms: Lateral Entry and Digitalisation

In 2018 the government introduced lateral entry at the Joint Secretary level, bypassing the UPSC for domain experts. The UPSC now conducts a separate selection process for such appointments. Digital initiatives — online application, e‑admit cards, computer‑based tests — have reduced cycle times. The 2022 amendment to the UPSC (Exemption from Consultation) Regulations expanded the categories of posts exempt from UPSC consultation, a move debated for its impact on merit‑based recruitment.

Examiner’s Twist

Questions may ask whether the UPSC is a "constitutional body" while the SSC is not. The answer: UPSC derives directly from Articles 315‑323; SSC is an executive body. Another trap is to assume that SPSCs are subordinate to the UPSC — they are independent constitutional authorities, though the UPSC can serve as a coordinating agency.

The Transition from the Planning Commission to NITI Aayog: Re‑configuring Federal Planning (2015‑Present)

Planning Commission: Structure and Fiscal Powers (1950‑2014)

The Planning Commission was established by a Cabinet resolution in March 1950, not by statute. It formulated Five‑Year Plans, allocated plan outlays to states, and monitored implementation through the National Development Council (NDC). Its Deputy Chairman held the rank of a Cabinet Minister, and the Commission wielded significant financial leverage because plan grants constituted a large share of central assistance to states.

NITI Aayog: Mandate, Governance and Absence of Financial Allocation

On 1 January 2015 the Government replaced the Planning Commission with the National Institution for Transforming India (NITI Aayog) through an executive resolution. NITI Aayog is a policy think‑tank; it does not allocate funds. Its Governing Council comprises the Prime Minister (Chair), all Chief Ministers, Lieutenant Governors of Union Territories, and select Union Ministers. The Vice‑Chairman and Chief Executive Officer are appointed by the Prime Minister. The absence of financial powers marks a shift from "plan‑centric" to "policy‑centric" federalism.

Cooperative Federalism: Governing Council and Regional Councils

The Governing Council meets periodically to discuss national development agenda, share best practices, and resolve inter‑state issues. Regional Councils, chaired by the Vice‑Chairman, address region‑specific challenges (e.g., North‑East, Himalayan states). This institutionalises bottom‑up input, contrasting with the top‑down plan allocations of the earlier era.

Key Initiatives: Aspirational Districts, SDG Index, and the Three‑Year Action Agenda

NITI Aayog launched the Aspirational Districts Programme (2018) targeting 112 under‑developed districts across 28 states, using real‑time data dashboards. The SDG India Index (first released 2018) ranks states on Sustainable Development Goal progress. The Three‑Year Action Agenda (2017‑2020) replaced the Five‑Year Plan framework, focusing on measurable outcomes rather than outlay targets.

Critiques and the Debate over Policy vs. Resource Allocation

Critics argue that without financial teeth NITI Aayog cannot enforce coordination, and that the Centre's discretionary schemes (e.g., PM‑KISAN, Jal Jeevan Mission) have re‑centralised fiscal decision‑making. Proponents contend that the think‑tank model encourages evidence‑based policy and avoids the rigidity of plan‑based allocations. The 15th Finance Commission's increased tax devolution (41 % for 2021‑26) is seen as compensating states for the loss of plan grants.

Examiner’s Twist

A frequent misconception is that NITI Aayog was created by a constitutional amendment. It was established by an executive resolution; no constitutional change was required. Another trap is to equate the NDC with NITI Aayog's Governing Council — the NDC was a statutory body under the Planning Commission, while the Governing Council is a new consultative forum.

The Model Code of Conduct and the Election Commission’s Enforcement Powers: Ensuring Electoral Integrity in a Federal Polity (1960‑Present)

Origins: 1960 Kerala Elections and the 1962 General Elections

The Model Code of Conduct (MCC) originated as a voluntary set of guidelines issued by the Election Commission (EC) for the 1960 Kerala Legislative Assembly elections. It was extended to the 1962 Lok Sabha elections and gradually acquired the status of a binding norm through the EC's consistent enforcement and judicial endorsement.

Legal Basis: Article 324, Representation of the People Act 1951, and EC’s Plenary Powers

Article 324 vests the EC with superintendence, direction and control of elections. Section 123 of the Representation of the People Act, 1951 defines corrupt practices. The Supreme Court in Union of India v. Association for Democratic Reforms (2002) and Election Commission of India v. State of Tamil Nadu (2019) affirmed that the MCC flows from the EC's plenary powers under Article 324, making it enforceable even without statutory backing.

Key Provisions: Campaign Silence, Use of Official Machinery, and Hate Speech

The MCC prescribes a 48‑hour "silence period" before polling, prohibits the use of government vehicles, personnel and premises for campaigning, bans announcements of new schemes after the election schedule is announced, and restricts hate speech and appeals to caste or communal sentiments. It also regulates opinion polls and exit polls.

Enforcement Mechanisms: Censure, Debarment, and Reference to the President/Governor

The EC can issue censure, bar a candidate from campaigning for a specified period, or recommend the President (for Union ministers) or Governor (for state ministers) to take disciplinary action. In 2019 the EC invoked its powers under Article 324 to restrict the Prime Minister's election rallies in West Bengal after the MCC came into force, a move upheld by the Supreme Court.

Judicial Scrutiny: The Supreme Court’s Endorsement in 2001 and 2019

In Election Commission of India v. State of Tamil Nadu (2001) the Court held that the MCC is not a mere guideline but a set of enforceable directions. The 2019 judgment reiterated that the EC's powers under Article 324 are "plenary" and include the authority to enforce the MCC against any person, including constitutional functionaries.

Examiner’s Twist

Students often claim the MCC is a statutory instrument. It is not; it is an executive instrument deriving legitimacy from Article 324. Another trap is to assume the EC can disqualify a candidate — it can only recommend disqualification to the President/Governor under Article 103/192. The EC cannot directly cancel a candidature.

  • Confusing the Bommai judgment's procedural safeguards with an amendment to Article 356.
  • Stating that the NJAC was struck down because it gave the executive a veto, rather than because it diluted judicial primacy.
  • Treating the UPSC and SSC as equivalent constitutional bodies.
  • Believing NITI Aayog was created by a constitutional amendment.
  • Assuming the Model Code of Conduct is a law passed by Parliament.
  • Thinking the Election Commission can directly disqualify a candidate without Presidential/Gubernatorial reference.
  • Equating the National Development Council with NITI Aayog's Governing Council.

The advanced sections above integrate constitutional provisions, landmark judgments, institutional evolution, and examiner‑focused nuances essential for high‑scoring answers.

The National Commission for Minorities: Constitutional Evolution, Mandate, and Role in Social Integration

The first Minorities Commission was created in 1978 by a resolution of the Ministry of Home Affairs as a non‑statutory advisory body. It was replaced by the National Commission for Minorities (NCM) under the National Commission for Minorities Act, 1992, which received Presidential assent on 17 May 1993 and came into force on 1 July 1993. The Act defines minorities as communities notified by the Central Government — Muslims, Christians, Sikhs, Buddhists, Parsis and Jains — and empowers the Commission to evaluate the working of constitutional safeguards under Articles 29 and 30, monitor the implementation of the Prime Minister’s 15‑Point Programme for the Welfare of Minorities, and inquire into specific complaints of deprivation of rights.

The Commission consists of a Chairperson, a Vice‑Chairperson and five members appointed by the President. It enjoys the powers of a civil court under the Code of Civil Procedure, 1908, including summoning witnesses, requiring production of documents and receiving evidence on affidavit. Annual reports are laid before each House of Parliament. Since its inception the NCM has produced influential reports on the 2002 Gujarat communal violence, the 2008 Kandhamal attacks, the 2013 Muzaffarnagar riots and the 2019‑2020 Citizenship Amendment Act consultations. Its recommendations have shaped policy on minority‑run educational institutions, scholarship schemes and the allocation of development funds under the Multi‑Sectoral Development Programme.

Judicial pronouncements have reinforced the Commission’s interpretative role. In T.M.A. Pai Foundation v. State of Karnataka (2002) the Supreme Court laid down the criteria for determining minority status at the state level, a principle the NCM applies when advising on Article 30 claims. The 2005 judgment in Islamic Academy of Education v. State of Karnataka clarified the autonomy of minority institutions in admissions and fee structure, prompting the Commission to issue guidelines for state governments. The NCM also participated in the 2019 Supreme Court hearings on the Assam National Register of Citizens, submitting data on the impact on minority communities.

Despite its statutory mandate, the NCM faces structural constraints. It lacks independent enforcement machinery; its recommendations are advisory and depend on executive acceptance. Vacancies in the Chairperson’s post — such as the 14‑month gap between 2014 and 2015 — have periodically hampered continuity. The 2019 amendment to the Act, which expanded the definition of “minority” to include any community notified by the Centre, was criticised for diluting the original legislative intent. Nevertheless, the Commission remains the primary institutional interface between minority communities and the Union government, and its reports are frequently cited in parliamentary debates and court proceedings.

For the UPSC aspirant, the key distinction is that the NCM is a statutory body under the 1992 Act, whereas the National Commission for Scheduled Castes and the National Commission for Scheduled Tribes derive their authority from Articles 338 and 338A respectively. Questions often test this contrast, the composition and powers of the NCM, and its role in the 15‑Point Programme. Remember that the Commission cannot adjudicate disputes; it can only recommend, and its recommendations are not binding on the government.

The National Commission for Women: From Statutory Body to Constitutional Recognition Debate and Gender Integration

The National Commission for Women (NCW) was established under the National Commission for Women Act, 1990, which was passed by Parliament on 30 August 1990 and brought into force on 31 January 1992. The Act provides for a Chairperson, five members (at least one from the Scheduled Castes or Scheduled Tribes) and a Member‑Secretary, all appointed by the Central Government. The Commission’s mandate includes reviewing constitutional and legal safeguards for women, recommending legislative amendments, investigating complaints of violation of women’s rights, and advising the government on policy matters affecting women.

Functionally, the NCW operates with the powers of a civil court: it can summon witnesses, require documents, and receive evidence on affidavit. Its annual reports are tabled in Parliament. Over three decades the Commission has intervened in landmark moments — the 1997 Vishaka guidelines on sexual harassment at the workplace (though the guidelines were issued by the Supreme Court, the NCW played a catalytic role in their implementation), the drafting of the Protection of Women from Domestic Violence Act, 2005, the Criminal Law (Amendment) Act, 2013 after the Delhi gang‑rape case, and the Sexual Harassment of Women at Workplace (Prevention, Prohibition and Redressal) Act, 2013. The NCW also drafted the 2017 National Policy for Women and submitted the 2020 report on women’s safety in public spaces, which informed the Nirbhaya Fund allocations.

A persistent demand has been to elevate the NCW to constitutional status, akin to the National Commission for Scheduled Castes (Article 338) and the National Commission for Scheduled Tribes (Article 338A). Private member bills and government‑sponsored proposals — most notably the Constitution (One Hundred and Twenty‑Fifth Amendment) Bill, 2019 — sought to insert a new Article 338B for the NCW. The Bill lapsed with the dissolution of the 16th Lok Sabha, and as of 2024 no constitutional amendment has been enacted. Consequently, the NCW remains a statutory body whose powers can be altered by ordinary legislation, unlike the constitutionally entrenched SC/ST Commissions.

The Commission’s effectiveness is also constrained by its advisory character; it cannot enforce its recommendations, and its budget and staffing are controlled by the Ministry of Women and Child Development. Vacancies in the Chairperson’s post — such as the 11‑month gap in 2017‑18 — have occasionally stalled inquiries. Nevertheless, the NCW’s suo‑motu cognisance of cases, its public hearings, and its role in shaping gender‑responsive legislation make it a critical node in the architecture of gender integration.

For examination purposes, remember the enabling statute (1990 Act), the composition (Chairperson + five members), the civil‑court powers, and the constitutional‑status debate. A typical trap is to conflate the NCW with the NCSC/NCST and attribute Article 338A to it. The correct answer: the NCW is statutory; only the SC/ST Commissions enjoy constitutional status under Articles 338 and 338A.

Examiner's Twist
A question asks whether the National Commission for Women enjoys constitutional status under Article 338A. How should you answer?
The National Commission for Women is a statutory body created by the National Commission for Women Act, 1990. Article 338A provides constitutional status only to the National Commission for Scheduled Castes and the National Commission for Scheduled Tribes. Several private member bills have sought to elevate the NCW to constitutional status, but as of 2024 no constitutional amendment has been enacted. Therefore, the correct answer is that the NCW does not have constitutional status; it derives its powers solely from the 1990 Act.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question contrasts statutory versus constitutional bodies, list the enabling legislation and the relevant constitutional article side by side. For the NCW, cite the 1990 Act; for the NCSC/NCST, cite Article 338 and 338A. This tabular comparison fetches full marks in GS‑II polity questions.

The Right to Information Act (2005) and the Central Information Commission: Transparency as an Instrument of Democratic Integration

The Right to Information Act, 2005 (RTI Act) was enacted on 15 June 2005 and came into force on 12 October 2005.
