Grievance redressal is institutionalised through a two‑tier mechanism: a District Grievance Redressal Officer (DGRO) and a State Food Commission (SFC) with powers to inquire, recommend penalties, and order compensation. The Act also provides for social audits, vigilance committees at the FPS level, and the disclosure of records under the Right to Information Act. Despite these safeguards, the Comptroller and Auditor General (CAG) reports (2022) highlight persistent inclusion‑exclusion errors (estimated 15‑20 % in several states), delayed SFC constitution, and inadequate storage infrastructure leading to grain damage.

The NFSA’s fiscal implication is substantial: the food subsidy bill rose from ₹1.15 lakh crore (2013‑14) to ₹2.87 lakh crore (2023‑24), reflecting both expanded coverage and higher procurement prices. The Act’s synergy with the Pradhan Mantri Garib Kalyan Anna Yojana (PMGKAY), which provided additional free foodgrains during the COVID‑19 pandemic, demonstrated the system’s surge capacity but also underscored the need for a more resilient supply chain and dynamic beneficiary databases.

Examiner's trap
A question asks: “The NFSA makes Aadhaar mandatory for availing foodgrains.” State whether the statement is correct and explain the constitutional position.
The statement is incorrect. The NFSA provides for Aadhaar‑based authentication but does not make Aadhaar mandatory; the Supreme Court in Puttaswamy (2017) held that Aadhaar can be made mandatory only by a law that satisfies the proportionality test. The NFSA itself is that law, yet the Court’s interim orders and subsequent amendments (e.g., the Aadhaar Act, 2016, as amended) require that no beneficiary be denied entitlement solely for lack of Aadhaar. Therefore, while Aadhaar is the preferred mode, alternative identification must be accepted.
Exam‑day strategy
When a question links a welfare statute to Aadhaar, first identify whether the statute itself mandates Aadhaar or merely enables it. Then recall the Puttaswamy proportionality test and the “no denial” principle. This two‑step check eliminates the common trap of assuming mandatory linkage.

The Integration of the Indian Urban Governance Framework: The 74th Constitutional Amendment, the Jawaharlal Nehru National Urban Renewal Mission, and the Smart Cities Mission

The Constitution (74th Amendment) Act, 1992, effective 1 June 1993, inserted Part IXA (Articles 243P‑243ZG) and the Twelfth Schedule, granting constitutional recognition to urban local bodies (ULBs) — Municipal Corporations, Municipal Councils, and Nagar Panchayats. It mandated direct elections, a five‑year term, reservation of seats for women (not less than one‑third), SCs/STs (proportionate), and backward classes (as per state law). The amendment also required the constitution of a State Election Commission (common with panchayats) and a State Finance Commission to recommend fiscal devolution.

Functional devolution is guided by the Twelfth Schedule’s 18 subjects (urban planning, water supply, public health, slum improvement, etc.), but actual transfer of functions, funds, and functionaries remains a state discretion. The 74th Amendment also envisaged Metropolitan Planning Committees (MPCs) for metropolitan areas (population ≥10 lakh) and District Planning Committees (DPCs) to integrate rural and urban plans.

The Jawaharlal Nehru National Urban Renewal Mission (JNNURM), launched in December 2005, was the first centrally sponsored mission to incentivise urban reforms. It operated through two sub‑missions: Urban Infrastructure and Governance (UIG) for 65 mission cities and Basic Services to the Urban Poor (BSUP) for slum rehabilitation. JNNURM tied central assistance to mandatory reforms — e‑governance, double‑entry accounting, property tax reform, community participation law, and the enactment of a public disclosure law. By its closure in March 2014, JNNURM had sanctioned projects worth ₹1.05 lakh crore, but implementation gaps persisted, particularly in reform compliance and operation‑maintenance sustainability.

The Smart Cities Mission (SCM), launched in June 2015, shifted from project‑based funding to a competitive “challenge” model. One hundred cities were selected through four rounds (2016‑2018) based on proposals for area‑based development (retrofitting, redevelopment, greenfield) and pan‑city ICT solutions. Each Smart City forms a Special Purpose Vehicle (SPV) — a corporate entity under the Companies Act — with 50:50 equity between the state/ULB and the Centre, headed by a CEO. The mission’s budget is ₹48,000 crore (central share) over five years, supplemented by state/ULB contributions and convergence with other schemes (AMRUT, Swachh Bharat, Housing for All). As of December 2023, over 5,000 projects worth ₹1.9 lakh crore have been tendered, with around 60 % completed.

Complementary to SCM, the Atal Mission for Rejuvenation and Urban Transformation (AMRUT, 2015) focuses on water supply, sewerage, storm‑water drainage, and non‑motorised transport in 500 cities, with a reform agenda overlapping JNNURM. The convergence of these missions illustrates an evolving federal architecture: the Centre sets outcome frameworks and provides catalytic funding, while states and ULBs retain execution autonomy. Persistent challenges include weak own‑source revenue (property tax collection averages 0.15 % of GSDP), fragmented institutional multiplicity (parastatals, development authorities), and the limited capacity of SPVs to integrate with existing municipal governance.

The Integration of the Indian Digital Public Infrastructure: The India Stack, the Unified Payments Interface, and the Open Network for Digital Commerce

The term “India Stack” refers to a set of open‑application programming interfaces (APIs) and digital public goods that enable presence‑less, paperless, and cashless service delivery. The foundational layer is Aadhaar (unique identity, 2009), providing biometric authentication and e‑KYC. The second layer comprises the e‑Sign framework (digital signatures via Aadhaar), DigiLocker (cloud‑based document storage), and the Unified Payments Interface (UPI), launched by the National Payments Corporation of India (NPCI) in April 2016. UPI standardised a single mobile application for inter‑bank peer‑to‑peer and person‑to‑merchant payments, leveraging the Immediate Payment Service (IMPS) rails. By March 2024, UPI processed over 12 billion transactions per month, valued at ₹18 lakh crore, making it the world’s largest real‑time payment system by volume.

The third layer includes sector‑specific stacks: the Account Aggregator framework (financial data sharing, 2021), the Open Credit Enablement Network (OCEN, 2020) for digital lending, and the National Health Stack (2020) for health records. The Open Network for Digital Commerce (ONDC), incubated by the Department for Promotion of Industry and Internal Trade (DPIIT) and launched in pilot in April 2022, aims to democratise e‑commerce by creating an open protocol for buyer‑seller discovery, logistics, and payments, reducing dependence on proprietary platforms. ONDC adopts the Beckn protocol, enabling interoperability across apps for mobility, food delivery, retail, and services.

Governance of this infrastructure is multi‑institutional: the Unique Identification Authority of India (UIDAI) for Aadhaar, NPCI (a not‑for‑profit company owned by major banks) for UPI, the Reserve Bank of India (RBI) for payment system regulation, the Ministry of Electronics and Information Technology (MeitY) for overall digital policy, and the newly constituted Data Protection Board under the DPDP Act, 2023 for data‑privacy oversight. The federal dimension arises because states are both data fiduciaries (e.g., land records, health data) and implementers of central schemes (PDS, PM‑KISAN) that ride on the stack. The Centre issues guidelines for API standards, security audits, and grievance redressal, while states adapt the stack for local language interfaces and last‑mile delivery.

Key outcomes include financial inclusion (over 500 million Jan Dhan accounts linked to Aadhaar and UPI), reduction in leakage in direct benefit transfers (estimated ₹2.2 lakh crore saved since 2014), and the emergence of a vibrant fintech ecosystem. Challenges remain: digital divide (rural broadband penetration ~37 % in 2023), cyber‑security incidents (CERT‑In reported 1.4 million incidents in 2022), and the need for a coherent regulatory framework that balances innovation with consumer protection across the layered architecture.

The Integration of the Indian Federal Education Architecture: The Right to Education Act, 2009, the Samagra Shiksha Abhiyan, and the National Education Policy 2020

The Right of Children to Free and Compulsory Education (RTE) Act, 2009, enacted pursuant to Article 21A (inserted by the 86th Amendment, 2002), made elementary education (classes I‑VIII) a justiciable fundamental right for children aged 6‑14. The Act mandates a 25 % reservation in private unaided schools for economically weaker sections (EWS) and disadvantaged groups, reimbursed by the state at per‑child cost. It prescribes pupil‑teacher ratios, infrastructure norms, and the constitution of School Management Committees (SMCs) with 75 % parent representation. Implementation is monitored by the National Commission for Protection of Child Rights (NCPCR) and State Commissions.

Samagra Shiksha Abhiyan (SSA), launched in 2018‑19, subsumed three erstwhile centrally sponsored schemes — Sarva Shiksha Abhiyan (SSA), Rashtriya Madhyamik Shiksha Abhiyan (RMSA), and Teacher Education (TE) — into a single integrated scheme covering pre‑school to senior secondary. It follows a “whole‑school” approach, providing composite grants for infrastructure, teacher training, digital initiatives (DIKSHA platform), and inclusive education for children with special needs. Funding is shared 60:40 between Centre and states (90:10 for North‑Eastern and Himalayan states). As of 2023‑24, the scheme covers 1.5 million schools and 250 million students.

The National Education Policy (NEP) 2020, approved by the Union Cabinet on 29 July 2020, is the first comprehensive education policy in 34 years. Its structural reforms include a 5+3+3+4 curricular design (foundational, preparatory, middle, secondary), universalisation of early childhood care and education (ECCE) by 2030, a shift to multidisciplinary higher education with multiple entry/exit points and an Academic Bank of Credits, and the establishment of a National Research Foundation (NRF) to fund competitive research. The policy envisions a “light but tight” regulatory architecture: a single Higher Education Commission of India (HECI) with four verticals (regulation, accreditation, funding, standard‑setting), replacing the UGC and AICTE.

Federal dynamics are central: education is a Concurrent List subject (Entry 25, List III). The Centre sets policy frameworks and provides catalytic funding through Samagra Shiksha and the PM‑Uchchatar Shiksha Abhiyan (PM‑USHA), while states retain legislative and administrative control over school education. The NEP’s implementation requires state‑level legislation (e.g., for the State School Standards Authority) and alignment of state curricula with the National Curriculum Framework (NCF‑SE, 2023). The policy also emphasises technology integration through the National Educational Technology Forum (NETF) and the Digital Infrastructure for Knowledge Sharing (DIKSHA), which are centrally developed but state‑deployed.

Challenges include the fiscal burden of the 25 % EWS reimbursement (states report arrears), teacher vacancies (over 1 million posts unfilled as per UDISE+ 2022‑23), and the need for capacity building of SMCs and newly created State School Standards Authorities. The Supreme Court in Society for Unaided Private Schools of Rajasthan v. Union of India (2012) upheld the constitutional validity of the 25 % quota, reinforcing the Act’s enforceability.

  • Assuming the 73rd Amendment automatically transfers all 29 Eleventh Schedule subjects to panchayats; devolution requires state legislation and is often partial.
  • Confusing the NFSA’s “priority household” entitlement (5 kg/person) with the AAY entitlement (35 kg/household) — they are distinct categories with different coverage.
  • Believing the 74th Amendment mandates the creation of Metropolitan Planning Committees in all cities; MPCs are required only in metropolitan areas (population ≥10 lakh).
  • Thinking UPI is a government‑owned payment gateway; it is operated by NPCI, a not‑for‑profit company owned by member banks, under RBI oversight.
  • Assuming the RTE Act’s 25 % quota applies to all private schools; it applies only to unaided private schools, not to aided or minority institutions (which have specific exemptions).
  • Mixing up Samagra Shiksha (school education) with the Higher Education Financing Agency (HEFA) or the National Research Foundation (higher education/research) — they operate at different levels.
  • Overlooking that the Smart Cities Mission’s SPVs are corporate entities under the Companies Act, not statutory municipal bodies, and thus do not replace elected councils.

The Transition from Planning Commission to NITI Aayog: Structural Shift in Federal Planning (2015‑Present)

The Planning Commission was set up by a Cabinet resolution on 15 March 1950 as an extra‑constitutional body to formulate Five‑Year Plans, allocate plan resources and monitor implementation. For four decades it exercised de facto financial authority over state plans through the Gadgil‑Mukherjee formula and later the Gadgil formula, making it the principal instrument of vertical fiscal transfers. The Commission’s composition — a Deputy Chairman of cabinet rank, full‑time members and ex‑officio ministers — gave the Centre direct control over plan priorities, often criticised for undermining state autonomy.

In 2014 the newly elected government announced the replacement of the Planning Commission by a think‑tank that would embody “cooperative federalism”. The National Institution for Transforming India (NITI Aayog) was constituted by an executive resolution on 1 January 2015; it has no statutory backing under an Act of Parliament. Its Governing Council comprises the Prime Minister as Chairperson, all Chief Ministers, Lieutenant Governors of Union Territories and select Union Ministers, ensuring a federal character absent in the Planning Commission. The Vice‑Chairperson, a Chief Executive Officer and full‑time members are appointed by the Centre, while part‑time members and special invitees bring domain expertise.

Unlike its predecessor, NITI Aayog does not allocate financial resources or approve state plans. Its mandate is advisory: to provide strategic and technical advice, foster competitive and cooperative federalism through initiatives such as the Aspirational Districts Programme, the Atal Innovation Mission and the Three‑Year Action Agenda (2017‑20), the Seven‑Year Strategy (2017‑24) and the Fifteen‑Year Vision (2017‑32). It monitors outcomes through the Development Monitoring and Evaluation Office (DMEO) and the NITI Aayog’s “Output‑Outcome Framework”. The absence of plan‑funding powers has shifted the fiscal lever to the Finance Commission and the Ministry of Finance, while NITI Aayog focuses on policy innovation, best‑practice dissemination and inter‑state learning.

Critics argue that the lack of statutory authority limits NITI Aayog’s enforceability and that the Centre continues to dominate through the Vice‑Chairperson and CEO appointments. The Supreme Court, in a 2018 public‑interest litigation, observed that the body’s advisory nature does not diminish the Centre’s constitutional obligation to consult states on matters of fiscal federalism. Nevertheless, NITI Aayog has institutionalised a platform for structured dialogue — the Governing Council meets at least twice a year — and has produced data‑driven indices (Health Index, Education Index, Export Preparedness Index) that inform both central and state policy.

The National Food Security Act, 2013: Federal Implementation and the Targeted Public Distribution System

The National Food Security Act (NFSA) was passed by Parliament on 10 September 2013, notified on 5 July 2013 and brought into force in a phased manner from 2014. It converts the erstwhile welfare‑based Public Distribution System into a legal entitlement for roughly 81.35 crore persons — 75 % of the rural and 50 % of the urban population. The Act creates two beneficiary categories: Priority Households (PHH) and Antyodaya Anna Yojana (AAY) households. PHH members receive 5 kg of foodgrains per person per month at highly subsidised prices (rice ₹3/kg, wheat ₹2/kg, coarse grains ₹1/kg). AAY households, identified as the “poorest of the poor”, receive 35 kg per household per month at the same prices.

Implementation rests on a federal architecture. The Centre, through the Food Corporation of India (FCI), allocates foodgrains to states based on projected offtake and reimburses the subsidy differential. States are responsible for identification of beneficiaries, issuance of ration cards, operation of Fair Price Shops (FPS) and grievance redressal. The Act mandates the establishment of a District Grievance Redressal Officer (DGRO) and a State Food Commission (SFC) to hear complaints. Transparency measures include the mandatory use of Aadhaar‑linked ration cards, electronic Point‑of‑Sale (ePOS) devices at FPS, and the publication of beneficiary lists on state portals.

Financial implications are substantial. The Centre bears the full subsidy cost for the allocated quantity, while states meet the cost of transportation, storage and FPS margins. The 2022‑23 budget estimated the food subsidy at ₹2.87 lakh crore, of which NFSA accounts for the bulk. States with limited fiscal space — particularly in the North‑East and hill regions — have sought enhanced central assistance for intra‑state movement of grains. The Act also provides for a “food security allowance” in case of non‑supply, payable by the state government.

Judicial oversight has reinforced entitlements. In 2013 the Supreme Court directed all states to implement NFSA within a fixed timeline, and in 2020 it ordered the universalisation of the “One Nation One Ration Card” (ONORC) portability to enable migrant workers to access rations across state borders. Persistent challenges include leakages (estimated at 15‑20 % in some states), exclusion errors due to outdated beneficiary databases, and the fiscal burden on states that have not fully digitised the supply chain. The 2021 amendment to the NFSA Rules introduced a “centralised grievance redressal portal” to standardise complaint handling across states.

Examiner's trap
A question asks: “Distinguish between the entitlements of Priority Households and Antyodaya Anna Yojana households under the NFSA.”
Priority Households receive 5 kg of foodgrains per person per month at subsidised rates (rice ₹3/kg, wheat ₹2/kg, coarse grains ₹1/kg). Antyodaya Anna Yojana households receive a fixed 35 kg per household per month at the same subsidised rates, irrespective of household size. PHH coverage is 75 % rural and 50 % urban population; AAY targets the poorest of the poor, roughly 2.5 crore households. The two categories are mutually exclusive and together constitute the NFSA’s legal entitlement framework.
Exam‑day strategy
Memorise the two NFSA entitlements (5 kg/person vs 35 kg/household) and the coverage percentages (75 % rural, 50 % urban). For NITI Aayog, recall that it is an executive body with no plan‑allocation powers — contrast with the Planning Commission’s financial role. In RTI, note the 30‑day/48‑hour timelines and the two‑tier appeal structure. For Lokpal, remember the exclusion of armed forces and the requirement for states to enact Lokayukta laws. For Smart Cities, the SPV is a Companies Act entity, not a statutory municipal body.

The Right to Information Act, 2005: Institutionalising Transparency across Federal Layers

The Right to Information Act (RTI) was enacted on 15 June 2005 and became operational on 12 October 2005. It applies to all “public authorities” — bodies established by the Constitution, by law made by Parliament or a State Legislature, or by notification of the appropriate government, including NGOs substantially financed by government. The Act mandates proactive disclosure under Section 4 (organisation, functions, decision‑making processes, norms, directories, budgets) and a request‑driven regime under Section 6.

A citizen may submit an application to the Public Information Officer (PIO) with a fee of ₹10 (waived for Below Poverty Line applicants). The PIO must respond within 30 days; where the information concerns life or liberty, the deadline is 48 hours. Exemptions are listed in Section 8 (national security, sovereignty, contempt of court, privacy, commercial confidence) and Section 9 (copyright). A “harm test” requires the authority to demonstrate that disclosure would outweigh public interest.

The appellate mechanism is two‑tier. The first appeal lies with an officer senior to the PIO within the same authority, to be filed within 30 days. The second appeal goes to the Central Information Commission (CIC) for central authorities or the State Information Commission (SIC) for state authorities, within 90 days. The Commissions, headed by a Chief Information Commissioner and up to ten Information Commissioners, have powers to impose penalties up to ₹25,000 on errant PIOs and recommend disciplinary action. Landmark judgments have shaped the regime: Central Board of Secondary Education v. Aditya Bandopadhyay (2011) held answer sheets are “information”; Namit Sharma v. Union of India (2013) mandated judicial members in the CIC; Anjali Bhardwaj v. Union of India (2019) directed timely filling of vacancies.

The 2019 amendment to the RTI Act altered the tenure and salary of the Chief Information Commissioner and Information Commissioners, aligning them with the Election Commission but removing the statutory protection of a five‑year term. Critics argue this weakens independence. Federal dynamics are evident: while the central Act sets the floor, states frame their own RTI Rules (fee structure, format, timelines for proactive disclosure). The Supreme Court in Union of India v. R. Gandhi (2021) affirmed that the central Act prevails over inconsistent state rules. Implementation challenges include massive pendency (over 3 lakh second appeals pending at CIC as of 2023), non‑compliance with proactive disclosure, and the use of “national security” exemptions to withhold routine information.

The Lokpal and Lokayuktas Act, 2013: Anti‑Corruption Architecture and Federal Implications

The Lokpal and Lokayuktas Act was passed on 18 December 2013, notified on 1 January 2014, and became fully operational only after the appointment of the first Lokpal in March 2019. At the Centre, the Lokpal consists of a Chairperson (a former Chief Justice of India or a Supreme Court judge) and up to eight members, at least 50 % of whom must be judicial. Its jurisdiction covers the Prime Minister (with safeguards: no inquiry on international relations, security, public order, atomic energy or space), Ministers, Members of Parliament, Group A and Group B officers, and employees of bodies funded or controlled by the Centre. The armed forces are explicitly excluded.

The Lokpal has an Inquiry Wing (for preliminary inquiry) and a Prosecution Wing (for filing cases in special courts). It can order search and seizure, provisional attachment of assets, and direct the CBI or any other agency to investigate. Timelines are strict: preliminary inquiry within 60 days (extendable by 60), investigation within six months (extendable by six), and trial in special courts to be concluded within two years. The Act also provides protection for whistle‑blowers who disclose corruption in good faith.

At the state level, the Act requires each state to establish a Lokayukta through its own legislation. The framework varies: Karnataka (1984), Maharashtra (1971) and Kerala (1999) have relatively strong Lokayuktas with suo‑motu powers, while several states have weak or non‑functional bodies. The Supreme Court, in Common Cause v. Union of India (2018), directed all states to appoint Lokayuktas within six months, yet as of 2023 a few states remain non‑compliant. The central law does not prescribe uniform powers for Lokayuktas, leading to asymmetry in anti‑corruption enforcement across the federation.

Operational challenges include the delayed constitution of the Lokpal (over five years), inadequate staffing of the Inquiry and Prosecution Wings, and the requirement for prior sanction to prosecute public servants — a procedural hurdle that the Act sought to remove but which persists for certain categories. The 2016 amendment to the Act extended the deadline for states to pass Lokayukta laws and allowed the Centre to notify the date of commencement. Despite these measures, the conviction rate in Lokpal‑referred cases remains low, and the institution’s deterrent effect is still evolving.

The Smart Cities Mission: Governance Model, Special Purpose Vehicles, and Federal Coordination (2015‑Present)

Launched on 25 June 2015, the Smart Cities Mission (SCM) aims to develop 100 cities through a competitive “Challenge” process. Cities submitted Smart City Proposals (SCPs) evaluated on vision, feasibility, impact and financial planning. The Mission adopts a “area‑based development” model (retrofitting, redevelopment, greenfield) complemented by “pan‑city” smart solutions (intelligent traffic management, e‑governance, sensor‑based waste management). Core infrastructure elements include assured water supply, 24×7 electricity, sanitation, efficient urban mobility, affordable housing, robust IT connectivity, good governance and sustainable environment.

Governance is anchored in a Special Purpose Vehicle (SPV) incorporated under the Companies Act, 2013 for each selected city. The SPV is a limited company with 50 % equity held by the Centre (through the Ministry of Housing and Urban Affairs) and 50 % by the State/Urban Local Body (ULB). The SPV’s Board includes the District Collector, Municipal Commissioner, representatives of the State Government and independent directors. The Chief Executive Officer (CEO) of the SPV, usually a senior IAS officer, exercises executive authority over project execution, procurement and financial management. This corporate structure is intended to insulate project delivery from political cycles and enable flexible decision‑making.

Funding follows a 50:50 central‑state share. The Centre provides ₹500 crore per city over five years (₹100 crore per annum), matched by the State/ULB. Convergence with other centrally sponsored schemes — AMRUT (water supply, sewerage), PMAY‑Urban (housing), Swachh Bharat Mission (sanitation), and the National Urban Livelihoods Mission — is encouraged to avoid duplication. As of March 2023, over 5,000 projects worth approximately ₹2 lakh crore have been completed or are under implementation, covering smart roads, integrated command‑and‑control centres, solar rooftops, and public bike‑sharing systems.

Federal coordination is operationalised through the Smart Cities Mission Directorate in MoHUA, which monitors progress via a real‑time GIS‑based dashboard (the “Smart Cities Geospatial Management Information System”). States retain flexibility in project selection within the approved SCP, but must adhere to the Mission’s guidelines on procurement, transparency (e‑tendering, third‑party quality audit) and citizen participation (ward‑level consultations). Challenges persist: SPVs often lack financial autonomy to raise market borrowings; ULBs face capacity deficits in project management; land acquisition for greenfield components encounters litigation; and the digital divide limits the reach of pan‑city solutions in low‑income neighbourhoods. The Mission’s second phase (2023‑2027) proposes a “City Innovation Fund” and greater emphasis on climate‑resilient infrastructure.

  • Confusing NITI Aayog’s advisory role with the Planning Commission’s power to allocate plan funds.
  • Assuming the NFSA covers the entire population; it legally entitles only 75 % rural and 50 % urban residents.
  • Mixing up Priority Household entitlement (5 kg per person) with Antyodaya Anna Yojana entitlement (35 kg per household).
  • Believing the RTI Act applies to private bodies; it covers only “public authorities” as defined in the Act.
  • Thinking the Lokpal can investigate the armed forces; they are explicitly excluded from its jurisdiction.
  • Assuming Smart Cities SPVs replace elected municipal corporations; they are corporate entities under the Companies Act, not statutory local governments.
  • Overlooking that Lokayukta powers differ across states because the central Act does not prescribe a uniform state‑level framework.

The Right to Information Act, 2005: Institutionalising Transparency and Federal Implementation

The Right to Information Act (RTI) was enacted on 15 June 2005 and came into force on 12 October 2005. It replaced the Freedom of Information Act, 2002 and established a statutory regime for citizens to access information held by public authorities. The Act defines public authority broadly to include any authority or body established by the Constitution, by law made by Parliament or a State Legislature, or by notification issued by the appropriate government. This definition brings central ministries, state departments, local bodies, and even substantially financed non‑governmental organisations within its ambit.

Institutional Architecture

  • Central Information Commission (CIC): Constituted under Section 12, headed by a Chief Information Commissioner and up to ten Information Commissioners appointed by the President on the recommendation of a committee comprising the Prime Minister, Leader of Opposition in Lok Sabha, and a Union Cabinet Minister.
  • State Information Commissions (SICs): Mirror the CIC at the state level; the Governor appoints the State Chief Information Commissioner and up to ten State Information Commissioners on the advice of a committee headed by the Chief Minister.
  • Public Information Officers (PIOs) and Assistant PIOs: Designated in every public authority to receive and process requests within 30 days (48 hours for life‑or‑liberty matters).

Federal Dynamics

Although the RTI is a central law, its implementation is markedly federal. States have the power to frame rules under Section 27, leading to variations in fee structures, appeal timelines, and the number of Information Commissioners. Some states (e.g., Maharashtra, Karnataka) have enacted their own RTI Acts that coexist with the central statute, while others rely solely on the central framework. The Supreme Court in Central Board of Secondary Education v. Aditya Bandopadhyay (2011) held that the RTI overrides the Official Secrets Act, 1923, reinforcing the primacy of transparency over secrecy across both tiers of government.

Key Exemptions and Judicial Interpretation

Section 8 enumerates ten categories of exempt information, including national security, sovereignty, and personal privacy. Courts have read these narrowly: in Girish Ramchandra Deshpande v. Central Information Commission (2013), the Supreme Court ruled that the exemption for personal information under Section 8(1)(j) cannot be invoked to withhold asset declarations of public servants. The 2019 amendment (RTI Amendment Act, 2019) altered the tenure and salary of Information Commissioners, sparking debate over institutional independence; the amendment was upheld by the Supreme Court in Anjali Bhardwaj v. Union of India (2021).

Operational Challenges

  • Vacancies in CIC and SICs often exceed 30 %, causing backlog of appeals.
  • Proactive disclosure under Section 4 remains patchy; many authorities fail to publish mandatory categories of information.
  • Digital divide limits online filing, especially in rural areas where physical submission is still prevalent.

The National Food Security Act, 2013: Coverage, Entitlements, and Federal Implementation

The National Food Security Act (NFSA) received presidential assent on 10 September 2013 and was notified on 5 July 2014. It aims to provide subsidised foodgrains to approximately two‑thirds of India’s population through the Targeted Public Distribution System (TPDS). The Act legally entitles 75 % of the rural population and 50 % of the urban population to receive foodgrains at highly subsidised prices.

Entitlement Structure

  • Priority Households (PHH): 5 kg of foodgrains per person per month at ₹3/kg for rice, ₹2/kg for wheat, ₹1/kg for coarse grains.
  • Antyodaya Anna Yojana (AAY) Households: 35 kg per household per month at the same subsidised rates, targeting the poorest of the poor.

Federal Implementation Framework

Foodgrains allocation is a central responsibility: the Food Corporation of India (FCI) procures, stores, and transports grains to state depots. States/UTs are responsible for identification of beneficiaries, issuance of ration cards, and last‑mile distribution through Fair Price Shops (FPS). The Act mandates State Food Commissions to monitor implementation and a District Grievance Redressal Officer for complaint resolution. Central assistance covers the full cost of foodgrains for PHH and AAY; states bear the cost of intra‑state transportation and FPS margins.

Technology‑Driven Reforms

To curb leakages, the government launched the Integrated Management of Public Distribution System (IM-PDS) and the One Nation One Ration Card (ONORC) scheme (2019). ONORC enables portability of ration cards across states, a critical feature for migrant workers. As of March 2024, 35 states/UTs have implemented ONORC, covering over 80 crore beneficiaries.

Judicial Oversight

The Supreme Court in People’s Union for Civil Liberties v. Union of India (2020) directed states to ensure universal coverage of NFSA during the COVID‑19 pandemic, leading to the Pradhan Mantri Garib Kalyan Anna Yojana (PMGKAY) which provided additional free grains. The Court also emphasised the duty of states to computerise beneficiary databases and conduct social audits.

Persistent Gaps

  • Exclusion errors: estimates suggest 10‑15 % of eligible households remain uncovered due to outdated census data.
  • Quality complaints: reports of sub‑standard grains and irregular FPS functioning persist in several states.
  • Fiscal burden on states for transportation and storage infrastructure remains a point of contention in Centre‑state fiscal negotiations.
Examiner's Technique: Federal Implementation Question
Question: "Analyse the federal challenges in the implementation of the National Food Security Act, 2013, with reference to beneficiary identification, portability, and fiscal federalism."
Answer: The NFSA creates a dual responsibility: the Centre procures and allocates foodgrains, while states identify beneficiaries and manage last‑mile delivery. First, beneficiary identification relies on state‑level Socio‑Economic Caste Census (SECC) data, which varies in quality and timeliness, leading to inclusion/exclusion errors. Second, portability through ONORC requires inter‑state data sharing and harmonised IT platforms; states with weaker digital infrastructure lag, undermining the migrant‑worker benefit. Third, fiscal federalism tensions arise because states bear intra‑state transportation, storage, and FPS margin costs without a matching increase in central transfers; the 15th Finance Commission recommended specific grants for PDS reforms, but disbursement remains uneven. Effective implementation therefore hinges on cooperative federalism — joint monitoring by the Central and State Food Commissions, standardised SECC methodology, and a predictable fiscal transfer mechanism.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for “federal challenges” of a central welfare law, structure the answer in three layers: (1) institutional division of functions (Centre vs. State), (2) data/technology interoperability across states, and (3) fiscal resources and transfer mechanisms. Cite the specific bodies created by the Act (State Food Commissions, District Grievance Redressal Officers) and any Supreme Court directions. This three‑layer template works for NFSA, RTI, MGNREGA, and similar statutes.

The Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code, 2016: A Unified Resolution Framework for Financial Integration

The Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code (IBC) was enacted on 28 May 2016 and became operational on 1 December 2016. It consolidated and amended laws relating to reorganisation and insolvency resolution of corporate persons, partnership firms, and individuals in a time‑bound manner. The Code replaced a fragmented regime comprising the Sick Industrial Companies Act, 1985, the Recovery of Debts Due to Banks and Financial Institutions Act, 1993, and the Securitisation and Reconstruction of Financial Assets and Enforcement of Security Interest Act, 2002 (SARFAESI).

Institutional Pillars

  • Insolvency and Bankruptcy Board of India (IBBI): Regulator for insolvency professionals, insolvency professional agencies, and information utilities. Established under Section 188.
  • Adjudicating Authorities: National Company Law Tribunal (NCLT) for corporate debtors; Debt Recovery Tribunal (DRT) for individuals and partnership firms.
  • Resolution Professionals (RPs): Licensed insolvency professionals who manage the debtor’s affairs during the Corporate Insolvency Resolution Process (CIRP).
  • Committee of Creditors (CoC): Comprises financial creditors; approves the resolution plan by a 66 % voting share.

Time‑Bound Resolution Process

The CIRP must be completed within 180 days from the admission date, extendable by a further 90 days with CoC approval. If no resolution plan is approved, the adjudicating authority orders liquidation. The Code also provides for a Fast Track CIRP (90 days, extendable 45 days) for small companies and start‑ups.

Cross‑Border and Group Insolvency

Sections 234‑235 enable cooperation with foreign courts and recognition of foreign proceedings, aligning with the UNCITRAL Model Law on Cross‑Border Insolvency. The 2020 amendment introduced a framework for group insolvency, allowing a single resolution process for financially interconnected corporate groups.

Federal Dimensions

Although the IBC is a central statute, its operationalisation involves state‑level DRTs and NCLT benches located in multiple states. The IBBI, a central body, sets regulations for insolvency professionals nationwide, ensuring uniform standards. However, state governments influence the infrastructure of DRTs and the appointment of Recovery Officers, creating occasional coordination gaps. The Supreme Court in Essar Steel India Ltd. v. Satish Kumar Gupta (2019) clarified that the CoC’s commercial wisdom is paramount, limiting judicial interference and reinforcing the Code’s creditor‑centric design.

Impact and Challenges

  • Recovery rates improved from an average of 22 % (pre‑IBC) to over 40 % in resolved cases (IBBI data, 2023).
  • Large backlog at NCLT benches; as of December 2023, over 15,000 cases pending.
  • Operational creditors (suppliers, employees) often receive lower payouts compared to financial creditors, prompting calls for legislative amendment.

The Competition Act, 2002 and the Competition Commission of India: Market Integration and Federal Regulatory Architecture

The Competition Act, 2002 (amended in 2007, 2009, and 2023) replaced the Monopolies and Restrictive Trade Practices Act, 1969. It aims to prevent practices having adverse effect on competition, promote and sustain competition, protect consumer interests, and ensure freedom of trade. The Competition Commission of India (CCI) was established in 2009 as a quasi‑judicial body with investigative, adjudicatory, and advocacy functions.

Prohibited Conduct

  • Anti‑competitive agreements (Section 3): Horizontal agreements (cartels, bid‑rigging) are presumed to have appreciable adverse effect; vertical agreements (tie‑in, exclusive supply) are assessed under rule of reason.
  • Abuse of dominant position (Section 4): Includes predatory pricing, denial of market access, and imposing unfair conditions.
  • Regulation of combinations (Sections 5‑6): Mergers, acquisitions, and amalgamations exceeding specified asset/turnover thresholds require CCI approval.

Federal Regulatory Coordination

The Act is a central law, but sectoral regulators (TRAI for telecom, IRDAI for insurance, RBI for banking) retain domain‑specific competition oversight. The CCI enters into Memoranda of Understanding (MoUs) with these regulators to avoid jurisdictional overlap. For instance, the CCI‑TRAI MoU (2014) provides for consultation on telecom mergers. States have limited direct role; however, state‑level advocacy is encouraged through the Competition Advocacy mandate (Section 49), where the CCI advises state governments on competition implications of policies.

Landmark Decisions

  • Google LLC v. CCI (2022): CCI imposed a penalty of ₹1,337.76 crore for abuse of dominance in the Android mobile device ecosystem; the NCLAT upheld the order with modifications.
  • Cement Manufacturers Association v. CCI (2016): Established the “parallel conduct plus” test for cartel inference.
  • Flipkart Internet Pvt. Ltd. v. CCI (2021): Clarified that deep‑discounting by e‑commerce platforms may constitute predatory pricing if below cost with intent to eliminate competitors.

Recent Amendments (2023)

The Competition (Amendment) Act, 2023 introduced a “settlement and commitment” framework allowing parties to resolve investigations without adjudication, reduced the threshold for gun‑jumping penalties, and expanded the definition of “control” for combination notifications. It also mandated the CCI to publish guidelines on penalty calculation, enhancing transparency.

Challenges

  • Resource constraints: CCI’s investigative wing (Director General) faces staffing shortages, leading to prolonged inquiries.
  • Judicial backlog at NCLAT and Supreme Court delays finality of orders.
  • Balancing competition enforcement with sectoral regulatory objectives remains a dynamic tension.

The National Education Policy, 2020: Curricular Integration, Federal Implementation, and Institutional Restructuring

The National Education Policy (NEP) 2020 was approved by the Union Cabinet on 29 July 2020, replacing the 1986 policy (modified in 1992). It envisions a holistic, multidisciplinary, and flexible education system aligned with the Sustainable Development Goal 4. The policy covers school education, higher education, and vocational training, and proposes a sweeping restructuring of regulatory architecture.

School Education: 5+3+3+4 Curricular Structure

  • Foundational Stage (5 years): 3 years of Anganwadi/pre‑school + Grades 1‑2 (ages 3‑8).
  • Preparatory Stage (3 years): Grades 3‑5 (ages 8‑11).
  • Middle Stage (3 years): Grades 6‑8 (ages 11‑14).
  • Secondary Stage (4 years): Grades 9‑12 (ages 14‑18) with multidisciplinary subject choices.

The policy mandates mother‑tongue/local language as medium of instruction until at least Grade 5, preferably till Grade 8. It introduces vocational education from Grade 6 with internships, and a National Curriculum Framework for School Education (NCFSE) to be developed by NCERT.

Higher Education: Multidisciplinary Institutions and Credit Architecture

  • Multidisciplinary Universities and Colleges: Aim to have at least one large multidisciplinary institution in or near every district by 2030.
  • Academic Bank of Credits (ABC): Digital repository for credit accumulation and transfer across institutions, enabling multiple entry/exit.
  • National Research Foundation (NRF): To fund competitive, peer‑reviewed research across disciplines.
  • Higher Education Commission of India (HECI): Single overarching umbrella body with four verticals — National Higher Education Regulatory Council (NHERC), General Education Council (GEC), Higher Education Grants Council (HEGC), and National Accreditation Council (NAC).

Federal Implementation Dynamics

Education is a Concurrent List subject. The NEP provides a national framework but leaves curriculum design, teacher recruitment, and medium of instruction to states. The State School Standards Authority (SSSA) will be set up by each state for transparent regulation. The policy encourages states to adopt the State Curriculum Framework (SCF) aligned with NCFSE. Financial support is channelled through Samagra Shiksha (centrally sponsored) and the Rashtriya Uchchatar Shiksha Abhiyan (RUSA) for higher education. The 2023‑24 budget allocated ₹1.12 lakh crore for education, with a significant share for NEP‑linked schemes.

Regulatory Transition

Existing regulators — University Grants Commission (UGC), All India Council for Technical Education (AICTE), National Council for Teacher Education (NCTE) — are to be subsumed under HECI. The transition requires legislative amendments (UGC Act, AICTE Act, NCTE Act) and coordination between the Ministry of Education and state legislative assemblies. As of 2024, the HECI Bill is under parliamentary scrutiny.

Equity and Inclusion Measures

  • Gender Inclusion Fund and Special Education Zones for disadvantaged regions.
  • National Scholarship Portal expansion for DBT of scholarships.
  • Technology integration through DIKSHA, SWAYAM, and the National Digital Education Architecture (NDEAR).
  • Confusing the RTI Act’s applicability to private bodies; it covers only “public authorities” as defined in Section 2(h).
  • Assuming the NFSA covers the entire population; it legally entitles only 75 % rural and 50 % urban residents.
  • Mixing up Priority Household entitlement (5 kg per person) with Antyodaya Anna Yojana entitlement (35 kg per household).
  • Believing the IBC allows unlimited extension of the 180‑day CIRP; the maximum extension is 90 days with CoC approval.
  • Thinking the Competition Act empowers state governments to adjudicate competition cases; adjudication rests solely with the CCI and NCLAT.
  • Assuming the NEP 2020 mandates a uniform national curriculum; it provides a framework while states retain curriculum autonomy.
  • Overlooking that the HECI Bill has not yet been enacted; UGC, AICTE, and NCTE continue to function under their existing Acts.

The 73rd and 74th Constitutional Amendments: Institutionalising Grassroots Democracy and Urban Governance

The Constitution (73rd Amendment) Act, 1992, which came into force on 24 April 1993, inserted Part IX (Articles 243‑243 O) and the Eleventh Schedule, listing 29 functional items for Panchayats. The Constitution (74th Amendment) Act, 1992, effective 1 June 1993, added Part IXA (Articles 243 P‑243 ZG) and the Twelfth Schedule with 18 municipal functions. Both amendments mandated a three‑tier structure for rural areas (Gram Panchayat, Panchayat Samiti, Zila Parishad) and three categories of urban local bodies (Nagar Panchayat, Municipal Council, Municipal Corporation) based on population size.

Key uniform features include: (1) direct elections to all seats; (2) reservation of seats for Scheduled Castes and Scheduled Tribes in proportion to their population, and not less than one‑third of all seats for women (including SC/ST women); (3) a five‑year term with elections to be held before expiry; (4) constitution of a State Election Commission (SEC) under Article 243 K/243 ZA to supervise elections; (5) a State Finance Commission (SFC) under Article 243 I/243 Y to review finances and recommend devolution; (6) devolution of powers, functions, and finances (the “3Fs”) as per the respective Schedules.

Implementation has been uneven. While all states have enacted conformity legislation, the actual transfer of functions remains partial. Many states retain parallel departmental agencies (e.g., water supply, primary health) that bypass Panchayats. Fiscal autonomy is limited: own‑source revenue (property tax, user charges) constitutes a small fraction of total expenditure; most funds flow as tied grants from centrally sponsored schemes. The SFC recommendations are often not fully implemented, and SECs sometimes lack adequate staff. The 2019 amendment to Article 243 ZD introduced a provision for cooperative societies to be elected to Panchayats, but its operational impact is still emerging.

Comparison of Rural and Urban Constitutional Frameworks
FeaturePanchayats (73rd)Municipalities (74th)
Constitutional PartPart IX (Art. 243‑243 O)Part IXA (Art. 243 P‑243 ZG)
ScheduleEleventh (29 items)Twelfth (18 items)
Tier/CategoryThree‑tier (village, block, district)Three categories by population
Reservation for Women≥ 1/3 of total seats≥ 1/3 of total seats
State Election CommissionArt. 243 KArt. 243 ZA
State Finance CommissionArt. 243 IArt. 243 Y

The amendments represent a constitutional commitment to decentralised governance, yet the gap between legal mandate and functional reality remains a central theme in federalism debates.

The Transition from Planning Commission to NITI Aayog: Reorienting Federal Policy Coordination

The Planning Commission was established on 15 March 1950 by a Cabinet resolution, not by statute. It wielded substantial financial powers: it allocated Plan funds to states, approved state Plans, and mediated inter‑sectoral priorities through the Five‑Year Plans. This centralised allocation mechanism often clashed with the constitutional finance architecture, especially the Finance Commission’s tax‑devolution role.

On 1 January 2015, the Union Cabinet replaced the Planning Commission with the National Institution for Transforming India (NITI Aayog) through another Cabinet resolution. NITI Aayog is envisaged as a “think‑tank” providing strategic and technical advice, without direct fund‑allocation authority. Its Governing Council comprises the Prime Minister (Chair), all Chief Ministers, Lieutenant Governors of Union Territories, and select Union Ministers, embodying cooperative federalism. Regional Councils address specific cross‑state issues.

Key functional shifts include: (1) from “plan‑based” to “policy‑based” approach – NITI Aayog formulates medium‑term frameworks such as the Three‑Year Action Agenda (2017‑2020), the Seven‑Year Strategy (2017‑2024), and the “India @ 2047” vision document; (2) monitoring and evaluation through the Development Monitoring and Evaluation Office (DMEO) and the Aspirational Districts Programme (launched 2018) which tracks 112 districts on health, education, agriculture, and infrastructure indicators; (3) fostering competitive federalism via the “State‑Level Performance Index” and “Good Governance Index”.

Critics note the absence of statutory backing, which limits enforceability of its recommendations. Overlap with the Finance Commission persists, especially on revenue‑sharing criteria. The 2022‑23 budget re‑emphasised NITI Aayog’s role in “outcome‑based budgeting” but the institution still relies on voluntary state cooperation for data sharing and implementation.

Planning Commission vs. NITI Aayog
DimensionPlanning CommissionNITI Aayog
Legal BasisExecutive resolution (1950)Executive resolution (2015)
Fund AllocationDirect Plan allocations to statesNo allocation powers
CompositionDeputy Chairman, Members, SecretaryPM + all CMs + UTs + Ministers
ApproachFive‑Year Plans, centralisedPolicy frameworks, cooperative
MonitoringPlan reviews, mid‑term appraisalsDMEO, Aspirational Districts, indices

The shift reflects a broader move from directive planning to facilitative federalism, though the practical balance between Centre and states continues to evolve.

Comparative Analysis Technique
Question: Compare the constitutional status, financial powers, and federal implications of the Planning Commission and NITI Aayog. Explain why the replacement was described as a move toward “cooperative federalism”.
Answer: The Planning Commission derived from an executive resolution (1950) and exercised direct Plan fund allocation, giving the Centre leverage over state priorities. NITI Aayog, also created by executive resolution (2015), has no allocation authority; it functions as an advisory body with a Governing Council that includes all Chief Ministers, institutionalising state participation. Financially, the Planning Commission’s allocations often bypassed the Finance Commission’s formula, creating tension. NITI Aayog’s role is limited to policy design, monitoring (e.g., Aspirational Districts), and fostering competition among states through indices. The federal implication: the Planning Commission embodied a top‑down directive model, whereas NITI Aayog’s structure — mandatory CM participation, regional councils, and absence of financial coercion — aligns with cooperative federalism, where Centre and states negotiate policy on a more equal footing. However, the lack of statutory backing for NITI Aayog means its recommendations remain non‑binding, a limitation not present in the Planning Commission’s de facto financial control.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for “federal implications”, structure the answer in three layers: (1) constitutional/legal basis, (2) financial/resource control, (3) institutional mechanism for state participation. Use a two‑column table in your rough sheet to contrast the two bodies; this speeds up recall and ensures you hit all comparison points.

The Evolution of Judicial Appointments: From Executive Discretion to Collegium and the NJAC Challenge

Under the original Constitution, the President appoints Supreme Court and High Court judges after “consultation” with the Chief Justice of India (CJI) (Articles 124(2) and 217(1)). The First Judges Case, S.P. Gupta v. Union of India (1981), held that “consultation” did not mean “concurrence”, affirming executive primacy. This enabled large‑scale transfers and appointments perceived as politically motivated.

The Second Judges Case, Supreme Court Advocates‑on‑Record Association v. Union of India (1993), reversed the position: “consultation” was interpreted as “concurrence” of the CJI, and a collegium of the CJI and the two senior‑most Supreme Court judges was mandated for Supreme Court appointments; for High Courts, the CJI and the two senior‑most judges of that High Court. The Third Judges Case (1998) expanded the Supreme Court collegium to the CJI plus four senior‑most judges, and clarified the consultation process.

In 2014, the 99th Constitutional Amendment and the National Judicial Appointments Commission (NJAC) Act sought to replace the collegium with a six‑member commission: the CJI (Chair), two senior‑most Supreme Court judges, the Union Law Minister, and two “eminent persons” nominated by a panel comprising the PM, CJI, and Leader of Opposition. The Fourth Judges Case (2015) struck down both the Amendment and the Act, ruling that they violated the basic structure by compromising judicial independence and the primacy of the judiciary in appointments. The collegium system was restored.

Since 2015, the Supreme Court has been negotiating a Memorandum of Procedure (MoP) with the government to introduce transparency measures — such as publishing reasons for selection, establishing a secretariat, and setting eligibility criteria. As of 2024, the MoP remains unsettled; the Court has issued directions for timely disclosure of collegium resolutions and for the government to act on recommendations within a defined period. Critics argue the collegium lacks accountability and diversity; proponents maintain it shields the judiciary from executive pressure.

Landmark data: between 1993 and 2023, the collegium recommended over 1,200 judges for High Courts and the Supreme Court; the government returned roughly 15 % for reconsideration, most of which were eventually reiterated and appointed.

Article 356 and President's Rule: Patterns of Use, Judicial Scrutiny, and the S.R. Bommai Doctrine

Article 356 empowers the President to assume the functions of a state government on receipt of a report from the Governor or otherwise, if satisfied that the state cannot be carried on in accordance with the Constitution. Between 1950 and 1994, President’s Rule was imposed over 100 times, often to dismiss opposition‑ruled governments (e.g., Kerala 1959, Punjab 1987, Uttar Pradesh 1992).

The Supreme Court’s landmark judgment in S.R. Bommai v. Union of India (1994) established critical safeguards: (1) the proclamation is subject to judicial review; the Court can examine the material on which the President’s satisfaction is based; (2) the majority test must be conducted on the floor of the Assembly, not by the Governor’s subjective assessment; (3) secularism is a basic feature, and a government pursuing anti‑secular policies can be dismissed; (4) the proclamation must be approved by both Houses of Parliament within two months, and can continue for a maximum of three years with periodic parliamentary approval every six months.

Post‑Bommai, the frequency of impositions dropped sharply. However, controversial uses persisted: Uttarakhand (2016) and Arunachal Pradesh (2016) saw President’s Rule imposed amid political crises; the Supreme Court in both cases ordered floor tests and restored elected governments. In Rameshwar Prasad v. Union of India (2006), the Court held that dissolution of the Assembly after a proclamation but before parliamentary approval is unconstitutional if the proclamation itself is mala fide.

The doctrine has been invoked in debates on federalism, with the Sarkaria Commission (1988) and the Punchhi Commission (2010) recommending stricter guidelines, including a requirement for a “reasoned” Governor’s report and a bar on using Article 356 for political gain. The 2018 Supreme Court judgment in NCT of Delhi v. Union of India reiterated that the Lieutenant Governor’s discretion under Article 239AA must not undermine the elected government’s mandate, echoing Bommai’s federal spirit.

The Integration of India's Defence Industrial Base: Ordnance Factories, Defence PSUs, and the Defence Acquisition Procedure

The Ordnance Factory Board (OFB), tracing its origins to 1775, functioned as a departmental undertaking under the Department of Defence Production. In October 2021, the government corporatised OFB into seven wholly‑owned Defence Public Sector Undertakings (DPSUs): Advanced Weapons and Equipment India Ltd., Armoured Vehicles Nigam Ltd., Gliders India Ltd., India Optel Ltd., Munitions India Ltd., Troop Comforts Ltd., and Yantra India Ltd. This restructuring aimed to improve autonomy, accountability, and competitiveness.

Other major DPSUs include Hindustan Aeronautics Ltd. (HAL, 1940), Bharat Electronics Ltd. (BEL, 1954), Bharat Dynamics Ltd. (BDL, 1970), Mazagon Dock Shipbuilders Ltd. (MDL, 1934), Garden Reach Shipbuilders & Engineers Ltd. (GRSE, 1884), Goa Shipyard Ltd. (GSL, 1957), and Hindustan Shipyard Ltd. (HSL, 1941). The Defence Research and Development Organisation (DRDO), established in 1958, serves as the R&D backbone, with over 50 laboratories.

Procurement policy evolved from the Defence Procurement Procedure (DPP) 2002, 2005, 2008, 2013, 2016 to the Defence Acquisition Procedure (DAP) 2020. DAP 2020 introduced the “Make‑I” (government‑funded), “Make‑II” (industry‑funded), and “Strategic Partnership” (SP) categories to promote indigenous design and production. The Innovations for Defence Excellence (iDEX) framework, launched 2018, engages startups and MSMEs for niche technologies. Two Defence Industrial Corridors — in Uttar Pradesh (2018) and Tamil Nadu (2019) — were notified to cluster manufacturing, testing, and certification facilities.

Challenges persist: technology gaps in aero‑engines, submarines, and advanced sensors; export control regimes limiting technology transfer; lengthy acquisition timelines (average 7‑10 years from Acceptance of Necessity to contract); and the transition of OFB employees to the new DPSUs, which raised service‑condition concerns. The 2023‑24 budget allocated ₹1.62 lakh crore for defence capital acquisition, with a target of 68 % domestic procurement by 2025‑26.

  • Assuming the 73rd/74th Amendments automatically guarantee fiscal autonomy; in reality, most Panchayats and municipalities remain dependent on tied grants.
  • Confusing NITI Aayog’s advisory role with the Planning Commission’s allocation powers; NITI Aayog cannot disburse Plan funds.
  • Believing the collegium system is constitutionally mandated; it evolved through judicial interpretation, not textual amendment.
  • Thinking Article 356 can be invoked solely on the Governor’s report; the President may act “otherwise”, but judicial review requires objective material.
  • Equating OFB corporatisation with privatisation; the seven new entities remain fully government‑owned DPSUs.
  • Overlooking that the Defence Acquisition Procedure 2020 replaced the DPP 2016, not the earlier DPP 2013.
  • Assuming the S.R. Bommai judgment abolished President’s Rule; it only subjected it to judicial scrutiny and procedural safeguards.

The Transition from Planning Commission to NITI Aayog: Re‑structuring Federal Policy Coordination (2015‑Present)

The Planning Commission, established by a Cabinet resolution in March 1950, functioned as the apex body for formulating Five‑Year Plans and allocating Plan assistance to states. Its dual role — planning and financial allocation — created a structural overlap with the Finance Commission, which is constitutionally mandated to recommend tax devolution. The Commission’s Deputy Chairman held cabinet rank, and its decisions bound state governments through the Annual Plan approval process. This centralised architecture was criticised for eroding state autonomy, especially after the 1991 economic reforms reduced the relevance of indicative planning.

In January 2015, the Union government replaced the Planning Commission with the National Institution for Transforming India (NITI Aayog) through an executive resolution, not a constitutional amendment. NITI Aayog’s Governing Council comprises the Prime Minister (Chairperson), all Chief Ministers, Lieutenant Governors of Union Territories, and select Union Ministers. Its mandate is advisory: to act as a “think tank” for cooperative federalism, monitor implementation of Sustainable Development Goals, and promote competitive and cooperative federalism through indices such as the Health Index, Education Index, and the Aspirational Districts Programme. Crucially, NITI Aayog has no power to allocate Plan funds; financial transfers continue to flow through the Finance Commission and centrally sponsored schemes.

The shift from a planning‑allocation model to a monitoring‑evaluation model altered the federal bargaining space. States lost a formal platform to negotiate Plan sizes but gained a forum for policy dialogue. The Three‑Year Action Agenda (2017‑2020) and the Strategy for New India @75 (2020) illustrate NITI Aayog’s attempt to set national priorities without financial leverage. However, the absence of fiscal teeth has led several states to demand a statutory backing for NITI Aayog and a clearer role in the GST Council’s compensation mechanism. The 2022‑23 budget retained the “Special Assistance to States for Capital Expenditure” scheme, administered by the Finance Ministry, underscoring that resource allocation remains outside NITI Aayog’s purview.

Examiners often test the distinction between the Planning Commission’s allocation authority and NITI Aayog’s advisory role. A common trap is to attribute the power to disburse Plan grants to NITI Aayog, which it never possessed. Another twist is to ask whether the Governing Council’s decisions are binding; they are not — they serve as consensus‑building instruments. The 2020‑21 Economic Survey explicitly notes that NITI Aayog’s effectiveness depends on the political willingness of the Centre and states to adopt its recommendations.

The Nationalisation of Coal Mines and the Creation of Coal India Limited: Energy Security and Federal Control (1971‑1975)

Coal, the backbone of India’s electricity generation, was predominantly in private hands until the early 1970s. The Coal Mines (Nationalisation) Act, 1971, brought coking coal mines under state control, followed by the Coal Mines (Nationalisation) Act, 1973, which extended nationalisation to non‑coking coal. The legislation was enacted under Entry 53 of the Union List (regulation of mines and mineral development), giving the Centre exclusive legislative competence. However, land acquisition and rehabilitation involved state governments, creating a federal interface that required coordination with state revenue departments.

The nationalised mines were initially managed by the Coal Mines Authority (1973) and later consolidated into Coal India Limited (CIL), incorporated in November 1975 as a holding company. CIL’s seven wholly‑owned subsidiaries — Eastern Coalfields, Bharat Coking Coal, Central Coalfields, Western Coalfields, South Eastern Coalfields, Northern Coalfields, and Mahanadi Coalfields — together account for over 80 % of domestic coal production. The move aimed to ensure stable supply for power and steel sectors, eliminate speculative hoarding, and enable planned investment in mechanisation.

Subsequent reforms reflect the tension between state control and market efficiency. The 1993 amendment to the Coal Mines (Nationalisation) Act permitted captive mining for private end‑use (power, cement, steel). The 2014 Supreme Court judgment in Manohar Lal Sharma v. Union of India cancelled 214 coal block allocations made since 1993, citing arbitrariness. The Coal Mines (Special Provisions) Act, 2015, introduced transparent auction of coal blocks for commercial mining, ending CIL’s monopoly. The 2020 Mineral Laws (Amendment) Act further liberalised the sector by removing end‑use restrictions.

From a federal perspective, coal royalty and district mineral foundation (DMF) collections are shared with states, but the licensing and regulatory framework remains central. The 2022‑23 budget allocated ₹1.62 lakh crore for capital acquisition in defence, but coal sector investment continues to be driven by CIL’s internal resources and central budgetary support. Examiners may ask whether coal is a state subject — it is not; the Union List entry gives the Centre legislative primacy, though states administer land and law‑and‑order aspects. Another trap is to conflate the 1971 and 1973 Acts; the former covered only coking coal, the latter all coal.

Examiner's trap
A question states: "NITI Aayog replaced the Planning Commission and now allocates Plan funds to states." Identify the error and explain the correct constitutional position.
The statement is false. NITI Aayog was created by an executive resolution in January 2015; it has no statutory or constitutional authority to allocate Plan funds. Financial transfers to states are made through the Finance Commission (tax devolution) and centrally sponsored schemes administered by line ministries. The Planning Commission, though extra‑constitutional, performed the allocation function until 2014. NITI Aayog’s role is advisory — policy formulation, monitoring, and fostering cooperative federalism. The Governing Council’s recommendations are not binding on the Centre or states.
Exam‑day strategy
When a question contrasts Planning Commission and NITI Aayog, first note the legal basis: Planning Commission (Cabinet resolution, allocation powers) vs NITI Aayog (executive resolution, advisory only). Then list three functional differences: (1) Plan formulation vs outcome monitoring, (2) Financial allocation vs no fiscal role, (3) Centralised decision‑making vs Governing Council with CMs. Use the phrase “cooperative federalism” only for NITI Aayog; the Planning Commission operated a “centralised planning” model.

The 74th Constitutional Amendment and the Institutionalisation of Urban Local Governance: Municipalities, Metropolitan Planning Committees, and State‑Level Implementation (1992‑Present)

The Constitution (74th Amendment) Act, 1992, inserted Part IXA (Articles 243P‑243ZG) to provide a uniform framework for urban local bodies (ULBs). It mandated three categories of municipalities — Nagar Panchayats for transitional areas, Municipal Councils for smaller urban areas, and Municipal Corporations for larger cities — based on population and revenue criteria. Seats are filled by direct elections; one‑third are reserved for women, with additional reservations for Scheduled Castes and Scheduled Tribes in proportion to their population. The Twelfth Schedule lists 18 functions, including urban planning, water supply, public health, and slum improvement, which states may devolve through legislation.

Two key metropolitan institutions were created: Metropolitan Planning Committees (MPCs) for every metropolitan area with a population of ten lakh or more, and District Planning Committees (DPCs) at the district level. MPCs prepare draft development plans, integrating spatial, economic, and environmental concerns; two‑thirds of their members are elected from the municipalities and panchayats in the metropolitan area, the rest are nominated. State Election Commissions (SECs) conduct ULB elections, and State Finance Commissions (SFCs) recommend devolution of taxes, duties, and grants-in‑aid. The amendment also requires states to enact conformity legislation within one year.

Implementation, however, has been uneven. Many states delayed elections beyond the five‑year term, citing delimitation or reservation issues. The Supreme Court in K. Krishna Murthy v. Union of India (2006) upheld the rotation of reserved seats but struck down arbitrary extensions. Fiscal autonomy remains weak: most ULBs depend on tied grants from the Centre (e.g., Smart Cities Mission, AMRUT) and state governments, with own‑source revenue (property tax, user charges) covering less than 30 % of expenditure. The 2018‑19 Economic Survey highlighted that only 12 states had functional MPCs, and DPCs were often non‑existent.

Examiners frequently test the distinction between the 73rd (rural) and 74th (urban) amendments. A common trap is to assume the 74th Amendment automatically guarantees fiscal autonomy; in reality, the amendment only mandates the creation of SFCs — it does not prescribe the quantum of devolution. Another twist is to ask whether MPCs have executive authority; they are planning bodies, not implementing agencies. The 2021 Model Municipal Law circulated by the Ministry of Housing and Urban Affairs attempts to standardise powers, but adoption is voluntary.

The Public Distribution System and the National Food Security Act: From Universal Rationing to Targeted Entitlements and Federal Implementation Challenges (1950‑2013)

The Public Distribution System (PDS) originated as a wartime rationing measure in the 1940s and continued after independence as a universal safety net. The Food Corporation of India (FCI), set up in 1965 under the Food Corporations Act, 1964, became the central procurement and storage agency. Until 1997, the PDS operated on a near‑universal basis, issuing ration cards to all households. The Targeted PDS (TPDS), launched in June 1997, introduced Below Poverty Line (BPL) and Above Poverty Line (APL) categories, with differential issue prices and quantities.

The Antyodaya Anna Yojana (AAY), started in December 2000, identified the “poorest of the poor” — initially one crore families — and entitled them to 35 kg of foodgrains per month at highly subsidised rates (₹2/kg for wheat, ₹3/kg for rice). The National Food Security Act (NFSA), 2013, converted these schemes into a legal entitlement. It covers up to 75 % of the rural population and 50 % of the urban population, guaranteeing 5 kg per person per month at ₹3/kg (rice), ₹2/kg (wheat), and ₹1/kg (coarse grains). Pregnant women, lactating mothers, and children aged 6‑14 years receive additional nutritional support.

Federal implementation divides responsibilities: the Centre allocates foodgrains to states based on NFSA coverage, transports them via FCI, and reimburses the economic cost. States are responsible for identification of beneficiaries, issuance of ration cards, doorstep delivery through Fair Price Shops (FPS), and grievance redressal. The “One Nation One Ration Card” (ONORC) initiative, rolled out from 2019, enables portability of entitlements across states using Aadhaar‑linked ration cards. The Supreme Court in People’s Union for Civil Liberties v. Union of India (2011) recognised the right to food as part of Article 21, directing states to universalise mid‑day meals and improve PDS transparency.

Leakages, inclusion/exclusion errors, and delayed digitisation remain persistent. The 2022‑23 Economic Survey estimated that 20‑25 % of subsidised grains still divert to open markets. Examiners often ask whether NFSA made PDS universal — it did not; it expanded coverage but retained targeting. Another trap is to attribute direct distribution to the Centre; the Centre only supplies grains to state depots. The distinction between TPDS (administrative) and NFSA (statutory) is a frequent source of confusion.

The Evolution of Judicial Appointments in India: The Collegium System, the NJAC Act (2014), and the Supreme Court’s 2015 Verdict on Judicial Independence

Until 1993, judicial appointments to the Supreme Court and High Courts were made by the President on the advice of the Council of Ministers, with the Chief Justice of India (CJI) consulted but not bound. The Supreme Court Advocates‑on‑Record Association v. Union of India (1993) — the Second Judges Case — reinterpreted “consultation” in Articles 124(2) and 217(1) to mean “concurrence”, establishing the Collegium: the CJI and the four senior‑most Supreme Court judges for SC appointments; for HC appointments, the CJI, two senior‑most SC judges, and the concerned HC Chief Justice plus two senior HC judges. The Third Judges Case (1998) clarified that the Collegium’s recommendation is binding on the executive, subject to limited grounds for reconsideration.

In 2014, the National Judicial Appointments Commission (NJAC) Act and the 99th Constitutional Amendment sought to replace the Collegium with a six‑member body comprising the CJI (Chair), two senior SC judges, the Union Law Minister, and two “eminent persons” nominated by a panel of the Prime Minister, Leader of Opposition, and CJI. The amendment aimed to introduce executive participation and civil‑society representation. However, in the Fourth Judges Case (2015), a 4‑1 majority struck down both the NJAC Act and the 99th Amendment, holding that they violated the basic structure doctrine by compromising judicial independence and the primacy of the judiciary in appointments.

Post‑2015, the Collegium continues to operate under a Memorandum of Procedure (MoP) that is being renegotiated between the judiciary and the government. The MoP governs criteria such as seniority, merit, diversity, and regional representation. Vacancies remain high: as of 2023, over 400 High Court posts were unfilled. Critics argue the Collegium lacks transparency, publishes no minutes, and has insufficient mechanisms for public scrutiny. The government has occasionally delayed appointments by returning names for reconsideration, leading to judicial‑executive friction.

From a federal angle, High Court appointments involve the Governor (on advice of the Council of Ministers) and the Chief Minister, but the Collegium’s primacy limits state executive influence. The Supreme Court has held that the Governor’s role is formal; the substantive decision rests with the Collegium. Examiners often test whether the Collegium is constitutionally mandated — it is not; it evolved through judicial interpretation. Another trap is to claim the NJAC Act was upheld; it was struck down. The 99th Amendment’s invalidation reaffirmed that Parliament cannot amend the Constitution to alter the basic structure, including judicial independence.

  • Confusing NITI Aayog’s advisory role with the Planning Commission’s allocation powers.
  • Assuming coal is a state subject; it falls under Entry 53 of the Union List.
  • Believing the 74th Amendment automatically guarantees fiscal autonomy for municipalities.
  • Thinking the NFSA made the PDS universal; it expanded but retained targeted coverage.
  • Claiming the Collegium system is constitutionally mandated; it originated from judicial interpretation.
  • Stating the NJAC Act was upheld by the Supreme Court; it was struck down in 2015.
  • Overlooking that the Planning Commission was replaced by an executive resolution, not a constitutional amendment.

The Evolution of the Union Public Service Commission and Merit‑Based Recruitment for All India Services (1950‑Present)

The Union Public Service Commission (UPSC) was constituted under Article 315 of the Constitution on 26 January 1950, inheriting the legacy of the Federal Public Service Commission established under the Government of India Act 1935. Its mandate is to conduct examinations for appointment to the services of the Union, notably the Indian Administrative Service (IAS), Indian Police Service (IPS) and Indian Forest Service (IFoS), and to advise the President on disciplinary matters, promotion and transfer of All India Services officers. The Civil Services Examination (CSE) has undergone three major structural shifts: the 1979 Kothari Committee recommendation introduced a common preliminary objective test; the 2001 Satish Chandra Committee replaced the single‑stage main examination with a two‑stage pattern (preliminary and main) and added an essay paper; the 2011 Baswan Committee further revised the syllabus, increased the weight of general studies, and introduced the Civil Services Aptitude Test (CSAT) as a qualifying paper. The UPSC’s autonomy is protected by Article 320, which requires consultation with the Commission on recruitment rules, promotions and disciplinary actions. In practice, the Commission’s recommendations are binding on the government except where the President, on the advice of the Council of Ministers, decides otherwise — a provision rarely invoked. Landmark judicial pronouncements such as Union of India v. R. Gandhi (1987) affirmed that the UPSC’s advice is not merely consultative but carries a constitutional obligation. The Commission also conducts the Engineering Services Examination, Combined Medical Services Examination and the Indian Economic Service/Indian Statistical Service examinations, thereby integrating technical expertise into the administrative cadre. Recent reforms include the introduction of a common eligibility test for Group B and Group C posts (2022) and the digitisation of the application‑to‑appointment lifecycle through the “One‑Time Registration” portal. Examiners frequently test the distinction between the UPSC’s advisory role under Article 320 and the executive’s power to make appointments under Article 309, and the fact that the UPSC does not recruit for State Public Service Commissions, which operate under Article 315 independently.

The Merger of the Railway Budget with the General Budget (2017): Fiscal Integration and Parliamentary Oversight

From 1924 until 2016, the Indian Railways presented a separate Railway Budget, a colonial legacy that insulated railway finances from the general fiscal framework. The merger, announced in the 2016‑17 Union Budget and effected from 1 April 2017, was based on the recommendations of the Bibek Debroy Committee (2015) and the NITI Aayog’s “Railway Reform” report.
